Completion for braided enriched monoidal categories
Scott Morrison, David Penneys, and Julia Plavnik

Abstract
. Monoidal categories enriched in a braided monoidal category 𝒱 \mathcal{V} are classified by braided oplax monoidal functors from 𝒱 \mathcal{V} to the Drinfeld centers of ordinary monoidal categories. In this article, we prove that this classifying functor is strongly monoidal if and only if the original 𝒱 \mathcal{V} -monoidal category is tensored over 𝒱 \mathcal{V} . We then define a completion operation which produces a tensored 𝒱 {\mathcal{V}} -monoidal category 𝒞 ¯ \overline{\mathcal{C}} from an arbitrary 𝒱 \mathcal{V} -monoidal category 𝒞 \mathcal{C} , and we determine many equivalent conditions which imply 𝒞 \mathcal{C} and 𝒞 ¯ \overline{\mathcal{C}} are 𝒱 \mathcal{V} -monoidally equivalent. Since being tensored is a property of the underlying 𝒱 \mathcal{V} -category of a 𝒱 \mathcal{V} -monoidal category, we begin by studying the equivalence between (tensored) 𝒱 \mathcal{V} -categories and oplax (strong) 𝒱 \mathcal{V} -module categories respectively. We then define the completion operation for 𝒱 \mathcal{V} -categories, and adapt these results to the 𝒱 \mathcal{V} -monoidal setting.



1.
Introduction

In
[
MP17
]
, the first two authors studied the notion of a monoidal category enriched in a braided monoidal category
{\mathcal{V}}
.
While the notion of a monoidal category enriched in a symmetric closed monoidal category has been extensively studied in the enriched category theory literature (e.g.
[
Str83
,
Kel05
,
Str05
,
GP18
]
),
the fact that the base for enrichment may be a braided monoidal category
{\mathcal{V}}
that is not symmetric has not been extensively explored. We note that Remark 5.2 of
[
JS93
]
foreshadows this development, and
[
BM12
]
studies an even more general setting,
of a category enriched in a duoidal category, which specialises to the present one when the two tensor products agree.
In
[
MP17
]
, we proved a classification result for
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal categories, which we improve slightly in §
6.4
below to obtain

Theorem 1.1
.

Let
{\mathcal{V}}
be a braided monoidal category.
There is a bijective correspondence

\left\{\,\parbox{199.16928pt}{\rm Closed ${\mathcal{V}}$-monoidal categories ${\mathcal{C}}$ such that ${\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{C}}\to-)$ admits a left adjoint}\,\right\}\,\,\cong\,\,\left\{\,\parbox{236.15796pt}{\rm Pairs $({\mathcal{T}},{\mathcal{F}}^{\scriptscriptstyle Z})$ with ${\mathcal{T}}$ a closed monoidal category and ${\mathcal{F}}^{\scriptscriptstyle Z}:{\mathcal{V}}\to Z({\mathcal{T}})$ braided oplax monoidal, such that ${\mathcal{F}}:={\mathcal{F}}^{\scriptscriptstyle Z}\circ R$ admits a right adjoint}\,\right\}.

(Note that the main result of
[
MP17
]
required ‘rigid’ in place of ‘closed’.)

Here,
{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}
denotes the
underlying category
of
{\mathcal{C}}
with the same objects and hom sets
{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\to b):={\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b))
.
We call
{\mathcal{C}}

closed
if every
{\mathcal{V}}
-functor
a\otimes-:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
admits a right
{\mathcal{V}}
-adjoint
[a,-]
.
We refer the reader to §
2.5
below or to
[
Kel05
, §1.11]
for a discussion about
{\mathcal{V}}
-adjunctions between
{\mathcal{V}}
-functors.
On the other side,
R:Z({\mathcal{T}})\to{\mathcal{T}}
denotes the forgetful functor, and by an abuse of nomenclature, we assume all oplax monoidal functors are
strongly unital
, i.e.,
{\mathcal{F}}(1_{\mathcal{V}})=1_{\mathcal{T}}
, and the oplaxitor morphisms
\nu_{u,v}
are isomorphisms whenever
u
or
v
is
1_{\mathcal{V}}
.

As in
[
MP17
]
, we adopt the convention that we write composition of maps from
left to right
, contrary to the convention of most of mathematics.
This has implications for other conventions, like our conventions for the internal hom in a closed monoidal category (Notation
2.1
) and the evaluation and coevaluation in a rigid monoidal category (Example
5.13
).
We also suppress all associators and unitors of monoidal categories to ease the notation.

Theorem
1.1
is somewhat surprising due to the presence of the adjective
oplax
, and the absence of any type of rigidity or pivotal structure.
In comparison, the article
[
HPT16
]
shows there is an equivalence of categories between

anchored planar algebras
in a braided pivotal category
{\mathcal{V}}
and triples
({\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{F}}^{\scriptscriptstyle Z},c)
where

{\mathcal{C}}
is a pivotal category,

{\mathcal{F}}^{\scriptscriptstyle Z}:{\mathcal{V}}\to Z({\mathcal{C}})
is a braided pivotal strong monoidal functor,
and

c
generates
{\mathcal{C}}
as a
module tensor category

[
MP17
]
.

It is thus natural to ask the question: if this braided oplax monoidal functor
{\mathcal{V}}\to Z({\mathcal{T}})
is in fact strong monoidal, what can we say about the corresponding
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal category?
In
[
MP17
, §1.2]
, we claimed this property is exactly that
{\mathcal{C}}
is
tensored
over
{\mathcal{V}}
.
To define the property of being tensored, we need
{\mathcal{V}}
to be closed to define the
self-enrichment

\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}
(see Examples
2.6
and
6.2
).
A
{\mathcal{V}}
-category
{\mathcal{C}}
is called tensored if every
{\mathcal{V}}
-representable functor
{\mathcal{C}}(a\to-):{\mathcal{C}}\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}
(see
[
Kel05
, §1.6]
or §
2.8
) admits a left
{\mathcal{V}}
-adjoint; notice this is a property of the underlying
{\mathcal{V}}
-category of a
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal category.
In §
7.2
below, we prove the following.

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
{\mathcal{V}}
be a closed monoidal category.
Under Theorem
1.1
, there is a bijective correspondence

\left\{\,\parbox{196.32384pt}{\rm Tensored closed ${\mathcal{V}}$-monoidal categories}\,\right\}\,\,\cong\,\,\left\{\,\parbox{236.15796pt}{\rm Pairs $({\mathcal{T}},{\mathcal{F}}^{\scriptscriptstyle Z})$ with ${\mathcal{T}}$ a closed monoidal category and ${\mathcal{F}}^{\scriptscriptstyle Z}:{\mathcal{V}}\to Z({\mathcal{T}})$ braided strong monoidal, such that ${\mathcal{F}}:={\mathcal{F}}^{\scriptscriptstyle Z}\circ R$ admits a right adjoint}\,\right\}.

We also get a bijective correspondence replacing closed with rigid on both sides above.

As a final application, we discuss the notion of
completion
for an arbitrary
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal category
{\mathcal{C}}
when
{\mathcal{V}}
is closed.
The completion
\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is a tensored
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal category which comes with a canonical inclusion
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal functor
{\mathcal{I}}:{\mathcal{C}}\to\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}
which satisfies a universal property.
There are interesting open questions related to this universal property, and we refer the reader to Remark
5.8
for more details.
We give many equivalent conditions under which
{\mathcal{C}}
is
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidally equivalent to its completion
\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}
in Theorem
8.5
.

Remark 1.3
.

When
{\mathcal{V}}
is symmetric, completion of a
{\mathcal{V}}
-category can also be thought of as a partial cocompletion, closing the representable presheaves in
{\mathcal{V}}-Fun({\mathcal{C}}^{op}\to\hat{{\mathcal{V}}})
under certain weighted colimits. We have not seen a development of this idea when
{\mathcal{V}}
is merely braided (or indeed a generalisation of weighted colimits to that setting). Even before passing to the case of
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal categories, this article takes a more pedestrian approach.

Indeed Ross Street has suggested to us that the monoidal structure we specify here on the completion of a
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal category should be thought of as the restriction of Day convolution on the entire category of presheaves.
At this point, we do not know how relaxed one can by about
{\mathcal{V}}
and have this work: when
{\mathcal{V}}
is symmetric and cocomplete, it should be straightforward, but we have not yet studied Day convolution on presheaves when
{\mathcal{V}}
is merely braided and so have not verified that Day convolution provides an alternative route to the completion of a
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal category.

1.1.
Tensored
{\mathcal{V}}
-categories

As mentioned above, being tensored is a property, and not extra structure, of the underlying
{\mathcal{V}}
-category of a
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal category, which we view as enriched in the closed monoidal category
{\mathcal{V}}
where we forget the braiding.
Thus to understand tensored
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal categories and the completion operation, we begin by working
one categorical level down
with ordinary
{\mathcal{V}}
-categories.

Versions of the following folklore theorems have been proven many times, and
[
Lin81
]
is the earliest appearance of which we are aware.
1
1

1

Other proofs appear in
[
JK01
]
,
[
GP97
]
and
[
GM10
, Lem. 4.7]
, all of which do not cite
[
Lin81
]
!

Theorem 1.4
.

Let
{\mathcal{V}}
be a monoidal category.
There is a bijective correspondence

\left\{\,\parbox{162.18062pt}{\rm${\mathcal{V}}$-categories ${\mathcal{C}}$ such that each ${\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\to-):{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{V}}$ admits a left adjoint}\,\right\}\,\,\cong\,\,\left\{\,\parbox{199.16928pt}{\rm Strongly unital oplax ${\mathcal{V}}$-modules ${\mathcal{M}}$ such that each $m\vartriangleleft-$ admits a right adjoint}\,\right\}.

Here, an oplax
{\mathcal{V}}
-module means the associators
\alpha_{m,u,v}\in{\mathcal{M}}(m\vartriangleleft uv\to m\vartriangleleft u\vartriangleleft v)
need not be an isomorphism, and strongly unital means that each unitor
\rho_{m}\in{\mathcal{M}}(m\to m\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}})
is an isomorphism, as is
\alpha_{m,u,v}
whenever
u
or
v
is
1_{\mathcal{V}}
.

Theorem 1.5
.

Let
{\mathcal{V}}
be a closed monoidal category.
Under Theorem
1.4
, there is a bijective correspondence

\left\{\,\parbox{113.81102pt}{\rm Tensored ${\mathcal{V}}$-categories}\,\right\}\,\,\cong\,\,\left\{\,\parbox{170.71652pt}{\rm Strong ${\mathcal{V}}$-modules such that each $m\vartriangleleft-$ admits a right ${\mathcal{V}}$-adjoint}\,\right\}.

It is also interesting to note that the article
[
Lin81
]
also points out that many results proven when
{\mathcal{V}}
is symmetric closed still hold when
{\mathcal{V}}
is merely braided closed!

Unfortunately, many of the above proofs available leave substantial parts to the reader.
In order to fully understand the operation of completion for
{\mathcal{V}}
-categories, we provide yet another independent proof of Theorems
1.4
and
1.5
in §
3
and §
4
respectively.
Along the way, we prove a helpful result on lifting underlying adjunctions to
{\mathcal{V}}
-adjunctions in Theorem
4.5
, which introduces the notion of a
tensored
{\mathcal{V}}
-functor
(see §
1.2
right below and §
4.1
for more details).

Now in the case of a closed
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal category
{\mathcal{C}}
for which the functor
{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{C}}\to-):{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{V}}
admits a left adjoint, the oplax
{\mathcal{V}}
-module structure of
{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}
can be easily written in terms of the classifying oplax monoidal functor
{\mathcal{F}}={\mathcal{F}}^{\scriptscriptstyle Z}:{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}
from Theorem
1.1
by
c\vartriangleleft v:=c{\mathcal{F}}(v)
and
\alpha_{c,u,v}:=\mathbf{1}_{c}\nu_{u,v}
.

1.2.
Completion for
{\mathcal{V}}
-(monoidal) categories

We discuss the operation of completion for
{\mathcal{V}}
-categories in §
5
.
Starting with a closed monoidal category
{\mathcal{V}}
and a
{\mathcal{V}}
-category
{\mathcal{C}}
,
we define the
objects of the
{\mathcal{V}}
-category
\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}
as the formal expression
a\blacktriangleleft u
for
a\in{\mathcal{C}}
and
u\in{\mathcal{V}}
, and we define the hom objects by
\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}(a\blacktriangleleft u\to b\blacktriangleleft v):=\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)v)
.
Similar to the self-enrichment
\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}
, the composition morphism
-\circ_{\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}}-
is given by taking the mate of

(\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)v}\mathbf{1}_{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(v\to{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c)w)})\circ(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{v\to{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c)w})\circ((-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)\mathbf{1}_{w})

under the adjunction

\displaystyle{\mathcal{V}}(\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}(a\blacktriangleleft u\to b\blacktriangleleft v)

\displaystyle\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}(b\blacktriangleleft v\to c\blacktriangleleft w)\to\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}(a\blacktriangleleft u\to c\blacktriangleleft w))

\displaystyle={\mathcal{V}}(\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)v)\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(v\to{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c)w)\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to c)w))

\displaystyle\cong{\mathcal{V}}(u\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)v)\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(v\to{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c)w)\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to c)w)

Here,
\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{u\to v}:{\mathcal{V}}(u\widehat{V}(u\to v)\to v)
is the unit of the adjunction
{\mathcal{V}}(uw\to v)\cong{\mathcal{V}}(w\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to v))

(notice our convention for internal hom is not the usual one).

We get a canonical
{\mathcal{V}}
-functor
{\mathcal{I}}:{\mathcal{C}}\to\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}
by defining
{\mathcal{I}}(c)=c\blacktriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}
and
{\mathcal{I}}_{c\to d}
is the mate of
\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(c\to d)}
under the adjunction

{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{C}}(c\to d)\to\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}({\mathcal{I}}(c)\to{\mathcal{I}}(d)))={\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{C}}(c\to d)\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{C}}(c\to d)))\cong{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{C}}(c\to d)\to{\mathcal{C}}(c\to d)).

Now when we assume our
{\mathcal{V}}
-category
{\mathcal{C}}
is tensored, we give many equivalent conditions under which
{\mathcal{C}}
is equivalent to its completion
\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}
in Theorem
5.12
.
We state a few of these conditions here.

Theorem 1.6
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{C}}
is a tensored
{\mathcal{V}}
-category.
The following are equivalent:

(1)

Every
{\mathcal{V}}
-representable functor
{\mathcal{R}}^{a}={\mathcal{C}}(a\to-):{\mathcal{C}}\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}
is tensored.

(2)

The
{\mathcal{V}}
-functor
{\mathcal{I}}:{\mathcal{C}}\to\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is tensored.

(3)

The
{\mathcal{V}}
-functor
{\mathcal{I}}:{\mathcal{C}}\to\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}
witnesses a
{\mathcal{V}}
-equivalence.

Suppose
{\mathcal{C}}
and
{\mathcal{D}}
are
{\mathcal{V}}
-categories, such that all representable functors for
{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}
and for
{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathcal{V}}
admit left adjoints. (In Definition
3.1
we called such categories
oplax tensored
.) In Definition
4.2
we define the notion of a
{\mathcal{V}}
-functor
{\mathcal{F}}:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{D}}
being
tensored
, and we sketch this here.
Given a
{\mathcal{V}}
-functor
{\mathcal{F}}:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{D}}

the underlying functor
{\mathcal{F}}^{\mathcal{V}}:{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathcal{V}}
can be canonically endowed with the structure of a strongly unital
lax

{\mathcal{V}}
-module functor between the
oplax

{\mathcal{V}}
-module categories obtained from Theorem
1.4
.
Indeed, we define the
laxitor

\mu_{c,v}^{\mathcal{F}}\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{F}}(c)\vartriangleleft v\to{\mathcal{F}}(c\vartriangleleft v))
to be the mate of

\eta^{\mathcal{C}}\circ{\mathcal{F}}_{c\to c\vartriangleleft v}
under the adjunction

{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{F}}(c)\vartriangleleft v\to{\mathcal{F}}(c\vartriangleleft v))={\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{F}}(c)\vartriangleleft v\to{\mathcal{F}}(c\vartriangleleft v)))\cong{\mathcal{V}}(v\to{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{F}}(c)\to{\mathcal{F}}(c\vartriangleleft v))),

where
\eta^{\mathcal{C}}
is the unit of the adjunction

{\mathcal{V}}(v\to{\mathcal{C}}(c\to c\vartriangleleft v))\cong{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(c\vartriangleleft v\to c\vartriangleleft v)
.
Now we say the
{\mathcal{V}}
-functor
{\mathcal{F}}
is tensored if
\mu^{\mathcal{F}}_{c,v}
is an isomorphism for all
c\in{\mathcal{C}}
and
v\in{\mathcal{V}}
.

As an interesting aside, we prove in Lemma
5.14
that a closed monoidal category
{\mathcal{V}}
is
rigid
if and only if every
{\mathcal{V}}
-representable functor
\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(v\to-)
is tensored.
As a corollary, we see that when
{\mathcal{V}}
is rigid, then a
{\mathcal{V}}
-category
{\mathcal{C}}
is tensored if and only if it is
{\mathcal{V}}
-equivalent to its
{\mathcal{V}}
-completion via the inclusion
{\mathcal{I}}:{\mathcal{C}}\to\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}
.

Returning to the
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal setting, when
{\mathcal{C}}
is an arbitrary
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal category, in §
8
,
we endow
\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}
with a
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal structure by defining

(a\blacktriangleleft u)(b\blacktriangleleft v):=ab\blacktriangleleft uv

and
the tensor product morphism
-\otimes_{\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}}-
as the mate of

(\mathbf{1}_{u}\beta_{w,\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)v)}\mathbf{1}_{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(w\to{\mathcal{C}}(c\to d)x)})\circ(\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)v}\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{w\to{\mathcal{C}}(c\to d)x})\circ(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}\beta_{v,{\mathcal{C}}(c\to d)}^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{x})\circ((-\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}-)\mathbf{1}_{vx})

under the adjunction

\displaystyle{\mathcal{V}}(\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)v)

\displaystyle\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(w\to{\mathcal{C}}(c\to d)x)\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(uw\to{\mathcal{C}}(ac\to bd)vx)

\displaystyle\cong{\mathcal{V}}(uw\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)v)\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(w\to{\mathcal{C}}(c\to d)x)\to{\mathcal{C}}(ac\to bd)vx).

In this setting, the
{\mathcal{V}}
-functor
{\mathcal{I}}:{\mathcal{C}}\to\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}
can be trivially equipped with the structure of a
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal functor.
Again as being tensored is a property of the underlying
{\mathcal{V}}
-category, we get the following formal consequence of Theorem
1.6
.

Corollary 1.7
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{C}}
is a tensored closed
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal category.
The following are equivalent:

(1)

Every
{\mathcal{V}}
-representable functor
{\mathcal{R}}^{a}={\mathcal{C}}(a\to-):{\mathcal{C}}\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}
is tensored.

(2)

The
{\mathcal{V}}
-functor
{\mathcal{I}}:{\mathcal{C}}\to\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is tensored.

(3)

The
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal functor
{\mathcal{I}}:{\mathcal{C}}\to\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}
witnesses a
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal equivalence.

As an example, which has many obvious generalizations, one may consider the category of vector spaces
{\mathsf{Vec}}
as enriched in super vector spaces
{\mathsf{sVec}}
in the trivial way which does not see the odd line.
The completion
\overline{{\mathsf{Vec}}}
is again
{\mathsf{sVec}}
.

Finally, in §
8.3
, we discuss the open question of whether
{\mathcal{C}}
being closed
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal implies
\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is closed.
This problem is similar in spirit to the open question raised in Remark
5.8
pertaining to the universal property of the completion.
Clearly any of the hypotheses of the above corollary imply
\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is closed, as it is then
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidally equivalent to the closed tensored
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal category
{\mathcal{C}}
.
We show that
\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is closed when
{\mathcal{C}}
is closed whenever
{\mathcal{V}}
is rigid in Proposition
8.10
below.

2.
Enriched categories

For this article,
{\mathcal{V}}
will denote a monoidal category.
As in
[
MP17
]
, we will always write composition of morphisms from left to right, contrary to the way most mathematics is written.
This results in other conventional differences which we address as they arise.
To ease the notation, we will omit writing tensor product symbols whenever possible and suppress all unitors and associators in
{\mathcal{V}}
.

Notation 2.1
.

When
{\mathcal{V}}
is closed, our convention for the internal hom
\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to v)
for
u,v:{\mathcal{V}}
is given by the following adjunction:

(2.1)

{\mathcal{V}}(uw\to v)\cong{\mathcal{V}}(w\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to v)).

The
evaluation morphism
or
counit

\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{u\to v}\in{\mathcal{V}}(u\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to v)\to v)
is the mate of
\mathbf{1}_{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to v)}
under the adjunction

{\mathcal{V}}(u\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to v)\to v)\cong{\mathcal{V}}(\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to v)\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to v)).

2.1.
{\mathcal{V}}
-modules

We briefly discuss module categories and module functors.

Definition 2.2
.

An
oplax
right
{\mathcal{V}}
-module category is a 1-category
{\mathcal{M}}
together with the following data:

•

a bifunctor
\vartriangleleft:{\mathcal{M}}\times{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{M}}

•

oplaxitor
morphisms

\alpha_{m,u,v}\in{\mathcal{M}}(m\vartriangleleft uv\to m\vartriangleleft u\vartriangleleft v)

for
m\in{\mathcal{M}}
,
u,v\in{\mathcal{V}}
,
and

•

distinguished morphisms
\rho_{m}\in{\mathcal{M}}(m\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}\to m)

satisfying the following axioms:

•

(naturality)
the
\alpha_{m,u,v}
and
\rho_{m}
are natural in all variables,

•

(associativity) for all
m\in{\mathcal{M}}
and
u,v,w\in{\mathcal{V}}
, the following diagram commutes (where we suppress the associator in
{\mathcal{V}}
)

\hbox to182.84pt{\vbox to47.17pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 89.1945pt\lower-22.16978pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

and

•

(unitality)
for all
m\in{\mathcal{M}}
and
v\in{\mathcal{V}}
, the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}v}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}\vartriangleleft v}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces m\vartriangleleft v}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces=}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{m,1_{\mathcal{V}},v}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho_{m}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{v}}

An oplax right
{\mathcal{V}}
-module category is called
strongly unital
2
2

2

In
[
MP17
]
we called this property ‘strictly unital’, but now prefer ‘strongly unital’.
if all morphisms
\rho
above are isomorphisms.
An oplax right
{\mathcal{V}}
-module category is called a
strong
right
{\mathcal{V}}
-module category if all morphisms
\alpha,\rho
above are isomorphisms.

Generally, the term ‘strong’ is omitted, and we merely refer to
{\mathcal{V}}
-module categories and oplax
{\mathcal{V}}
-module categories.
There is a similar notion of a(n oplax) left
{\mathcal{V}}
-module category.

Definition 2.3
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{M}},{\mathcal{N}}
are right oplax
{\mathcal{V}}
-modules.
A
lax
{\mathcal{V}}
-module functor
{\mathcal{M}}\to{\mathcal{N}}
is a pair
({\mathcal{F}},\mu)
where
{\mathcal{F}}:{\mathcal{M}}\to{\mathcal{N}}
is a functor and
\mu
is a family of maps

\{\mu_{m,v}\in{\mathcal{N}}({\mathcal{F}}(m)\vartriangleleft v\to{\mathcal{F}}(m\vartriangleleft v))\}

such that

•

(naturality)

\mu_{m,v}
is natural in
m\in{\mathcal{M}}
and
v\in{\mathcal{V}}
,

•

(associativity)
for all
m\in{\mathcal{M}}
and
u,v\in{\mathcal{V}}
,

\mu_{m,uv}\circ{\mathcal{F}}(\alpha^{\mathcal{C}}_{m,u,v})=\alpha^{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathcal{F}}(m),u,v}\circ(\mu_{m,u}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{v})\circ\mu_{m\vartriangleleft u,v}
as morphisms in
{\mathcal{N}}({\mathcal{F}}(m)\vartriangleleft uv\to{\mathcal{F}}(m\vartriangleleft u\vartriangleleft v))
, and

•

(unitality)
for all
m\in{\mathcal{M}}
,
\mu_{m,1_{\mathcal{V}}}\circ{\mathcal{F}}(\rho^{\mathcal{M}}_{m})=\rho^{\mathcal{N}}_{m}\in{\mathcal{N}}({\mathcal{F}}(m)\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{F}}(m))
.

A lax
{\mathcal{V}}
-module functor is called
strongly unital
if every
\mu_{m,1_{\mathcal{V}}}
is an isomorphism.
A lax
{\mathcal{V}}
-module functor is called a
(strong)
{\mathcal{V}}
-module functor
if every
\mu_{m,v}
is an isomorphism.

An
equivalence
between oplax
{\mathcal{V}}
-modules consists of a pair of strong
{\mathcal{V}}
-module functors between oplax
{\mathcal{V}}
-modules which is an equivalence of the underlying categories.

Remark 2.4
.

If
{\mathcal{M}}
and
{\mathcal{N}}
are strongly unital so that
\rho^{\mathcal{M}}_{m}
and
\rho^{\mathcal{N}}_{n}
are isomorphisms for
m\in{\mathcal{M}}
and
n\in{\mathcal{N}}
, then any lax
{\mathcal{V}}
-module functor
{\mathcal{F}}
is automatically strongly unital.
Observe two out of three morphisms in the unital relation being invertible implies the third is invertible as well.

2.2.
{\mathcal{V}}
-categories,
{\mathcal{V}}
-functors, and
{\mathcal{V}}
-natural transformations

We now recall the basics of enriched categories from
[
Kel05
, §1]
.

Definition 2.5
.

A
{\mathcal{V}}
-category
{\mathcal{C}}
consists of a collection of objects,
an assignment of a
hom object

{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)
to every pair of objects
a,b\in{\mathcal{C}}
,
to each object
a\in{\mathcal{C}}
, an
identity element

j_{a}\in{\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to a))
,
and to each triple of objects
a,b,c\in{\mathcal{C}}
, a
composition morphism

-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-\in{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{C}}(a\to b){\mathcal{C}}(b\to c)\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to c))
.
The composition morphisms must be associative, and the identity element must satisfy
(j_{a}\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)=\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}=(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}j_{b})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)
for all
a,b\in{\mathcal{C}}
.

Example 2.6

(Self enrichment)
.

When
{\mathcal{V}}
is closed, we may define the self enriched category
\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}
, which is
{\mathcal{V}}
thought of as a
{\mathcal{V}}
-category.
The objects are the same as before, and hom objects are given by internal hom
\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to v)
.
The composition map
-\circ_{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}-:\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to v)\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(v\to w)\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to w)
is the mate of
(\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{u\to v}\mathbf{1}_{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(v\to w)})\circ\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{v\to w}
under the adjunction

{\mathcal{V}}(u\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to v)\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(v\to w)\to w)\cong{\mathcal{V}}(\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to v)\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(v\to w)\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to w)).

Definition 2.7
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}}
are
{\mathcal{V}}
-categories.
A
{\mathcal{V}}
-functor
{\mathcal{F}}:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{D}}
is a function on objects together with an assignment of a morphism
{\mathcal{F}}_{a\to b}\in{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)\to{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{F}}(a)\to{\mathcal{F}}(b)))
to each pair of objects
a,b\in{\mathcal{C}}
such that
({\mathcal{F}}_{a\to b}{\mathcal{F}}_{b\to c})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-)=(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)\circ{\mathcal{F}}_{a\to c}
.

Example 2.8

(
{\mathcal{V}}
-representable functors)
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{C}}
is a
{\mathcal{V}}
-category with
{\mathcal{V}}
closed and
a\in{\mathcal{C}}
.
We define the
{\mathcal{V}}
-
representable functor
3
3

3

Kelly defines
{\mathcal{V}}
-representable functors in
[
Kel05
, §1.6]
under the assumption
{\mathcal{V}}
is symmetric, but this assumption is not necessary.
Indeed,
{\mathcal{V}}
does not even need to be braided, merely monoidal.
This is also observed by
[
Lin81
]
.

{\mathcal{C}}(a\to-):{\mathcal{C}}\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}
on objects by
b\mapsto{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)
and
{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)_{b\to c}
is the mate of
-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-
under the adjunction

{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{C}}(b\to c)\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}({\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to c)))\cong{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{C}}(a\to b){\mathcal{C}}(b\to c)\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to c)).

Suppose
{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}}:{\mathcal{D}}\to{\mathcal{D}}
are
{\mathcal{V}}
-functors.

Definition 2.9
.

A
1_{\mathcal{V}}
-graded
{\mathcal{V}}
-natural transformation
\sigma:{\mathcal{F}}\Rightarrow{\mathcal{G}}
is a collection of morphisms
\sigma_{a}\in{\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{F}}(a)\to{\mathcal{G}}(a)))
such that for all
a,b\in{\mathcal{C}}
, the following diagram commutes:

(2.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{F}}(a)\to{\mathcal{G}}(a)){\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{G}}(a)\to{\mathcal{G}}(b))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{F}}(a)\to{\mathcal{F}}(b)){\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{F}}(b)\to{\mathcal{G}}(b))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{F}}(a)\to{\mathcal{G}}(b)).}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{a}{\mathcal{G}}_{a\to b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}_{a\to b}\sigma_{b}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-}

2.3.
The underlying category/functor

Let
{\mathcal{C}}
be a
{\mathcal{V}}
-category.

Definition 2.10
.

The
underlying category

{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}
of
{\mathcal{C}}
has the same objects as
{\mathcal{C}}
, and the morphism sets are given by
{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\to b)={\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b))
.
The identity in
{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\to a)={\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to a))
is
j_{a}
, and composition is given for
f\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\to b)
and
g\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(b\to c)
by
f\circ g=(fg)\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)
.
It is straightforward to verify using the axioms of a
{\mathcal{V}}
-category that
{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}
is an ordinary category.

Example 2.11
.

When
{\mathcal{V}}
is closed, the underlying category
\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}^{\mathcal{V}}
can be identified with
{\mathcal{V}}
under the adjunction

\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}^{\mathcal{V}}(u\to v)={\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to[u,v])\cong{\mathcal{V}}(u\to v).

Suppose now
{\mathcal{F}}:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{D}}
is a
{\mathcal{V}}
-functor between
{\mathcal{V}}
-categories.

Definition 2.12
.

The
underlying functor

{\mathcal{F}}^{\mathcal{V}}:{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathcal{V}}
is defined on objects by
{\mathcal{F}}^{\mathcal{V}}(c)={\mathcal{F}}(c)
and on morphims by mapping
f\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\to b)
to
f\circ{\mathcal{F}}_{a\to b}\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{F}}(a)\to{\mathcal{F}}(b))
.
It is straightforward to verify using the axioms of a
{\mathcal{V}}
-functor that
{\mathcal{F}}^{\mathcal{V}}
is an ordinary functor.

Example 2.13

(Representable functors)
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{C}}
is a
{\mathcal{V}}
-category and
a\in{\mathcal{C}}
.
We define the
representable functor

{\mathcal{R}}_{a}={\mathcal{C}}(a\to-):{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{V}}
on objects by
b\mapsto{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)
and on morphisms
f\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(b\to c)={\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c))
by
{\mathcal{R}}_{a}(f)=(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}f)\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)
.
It is straightforward to verify tht
{\mathcal{R}}_{a}
is a functor using the axioms of a
{\mathcal{V}}
-category.

Lemma 2.14
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{V}}
is closed,
{\mathcal{C}}
is a
{\mathcal{V}}
-category, and
a\in{\mathcal{C}}
.
Under the identification of
\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}^{\mathcal{V}}={\mathcal{V}}
in Example
2.11
,
the underlying functor of the
{\mathcal{V}}
-representable functor
{\mathcal{R}}^{a}:{\mathcal{C}}\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}
is equal to the representable functor
{\mathcal{R}}_{a}=({\mathcal{R}}^{a})^{\mathcal{V}}:{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{V}}
.

Proof.

On objects, we have
({\mathcal{R}}^{a})^{\mathcal{V}}(b)={\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)={\mathcal{R}}_{a}(b)
.
If
f\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(b\to c)={\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b))
,

({\mathcal{R}}^{a})^{\mathcal{V}}(f)
is the mate of
f\circ{\mathcal{R}}^{a}_{b\to c}
under the adjunction

\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}^{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to c))={\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}({\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to c)))\cong{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to c)),

which is exactly
(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}f)\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)={\mathcal{R}}_{a}(f)
by Example
2.8
.
∎

Suppose that
{\mathcal{F}},{\mathcal{G}}:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{D}}
are
{\mathcal{V}}
-functors and
\sigma:{\mathcal{F}}\Rightarrow{\mathcal{G}}
is a
{\mathcal{V}}
-natural transformation.

Definition 2.15
.

The
underlying natural transformation

\sigma^{\mathcal{V}}:{\mathcal{F}}^{\mathcal{V}}\Rightarrow{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathcal{V}}
is defined by

\sigma^{\mathcal{V}}_{a}=\sigma_{a}\in{\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{F}}(a)\to{\mathcal{G}}(a)))={\mathcal{D}}^{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{F}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a)\to{\mathcal{G}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a))
.
It is straightforward to verify using (
2.2
) that
\sigma^{\mathcal{V}}
is an ordinary natural transformation.

2.4.
Representable functors, mates, and the Yoneda Lemma

Suppose
{\mathcal{C}}
and
{\mathcal{D}}
are ordinary categories,
{\mathcal{L}}:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{D}}
and
{\mathcal{R}}:{\mathcal{D}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
are functors, and
{\mathcal{L}}
is left adjoint to
{\mathcal{R}}
, denoted
{\mathcal{L}}\dashv{\mathcal{R}}
.
This means for all
a\in{\mathcal{C}}
and
d\in{\mathcal{D}}
, we have a natural isomorphism

(2.3)

{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)\to d)\cong{\mathcal{C}}(a\to{\mathcal{R}}(d)).

For
f\in{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)\to d)
and
g\in{\mathcal{C}}(a\to{\mathcal{R}}(d))
, we say
f
is the mate of
g
if
g
is corresponds to
f
under the above natural isomorphism.
We also say
g
is the mate of
f
.

The following helpful identities hold via naturality.

(2.4)

If
f_{1}\in{\mathcal{C}}(a\to{\mathcal{R}}(c))
and
f_{2}\in{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{R}}(c)\to{\mathcal{R}}(d))
, we have
\mate(f_{1}\circ f_{2})={\mathcal{L}}(f_{1})\circ\mate(f_{2})
.

(2.5)

If
g_{1}\in{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)\to{\mathcal{L}}(b))
and
g_{2}\in{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{L}}(b)\to d)
, we have
\mate(g_{1}\circ g_{2})=\mate(g_{1})\circ{\mathcal{R}}(g_{2})
.

The
counit
of the adjunction
{\mathcal{L}}\dashv{\mathcal{R}}
is the natural isomorphism
\eta:\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{C}}\Rightarrow{\mathcal{L}}\circ{\mathcal{R}}
defined by
\eta_{a}\in{\mathcal{C}}(a\to{\mathcal{R}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)))\cong{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)\to{\mathcal{L}}(a))
is the mate of
\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{L}}(a)}
.
The
unit
of the adjunction is the natural isomorphism
\varepsilon:{\mathcal{R}}\circ{\mathcal{L}}\Rightarrow\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{D}}
where
\varepsilon_{d}\in{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{R}}(d))\to d)\cong{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{R}}(d)\to{\mathcal{R}}(d))
is the mate of
\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{R}}(d)}
.

The Yoneda lemma gives fully faithful functors
{\mathcal{C}}\hookrightarrow{\mathsf{Fun}}({\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathsf{Set}})
by
c\mapsto{\mathcal{C}}(c\to-)
and
{\mathcal{C}}\hookrightarrow{\mathsf{Fun}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\op}\to{\mathsf{Set}})
by
c\mapsto{\mathcal{C}}(-\to c)
.
Recall that a functor
{\mathcal{F}}:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathsf{Set}}
is called
representable
if there is a pair
(c,\gamma)
consisting of a representing object
c\in{\mathcal{C}}
and a natural isomorphism
\gamma:{\mathcal{F}}\Rightarrow{\mathcal{C}}(c\to-)
.
Given any two representations
(a,\alpha)
and
(b,\beta)
of
{\mathcal{F}}
, there is a canonical isomorphism
f\in{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)
such that
{\mathcal{C}}(f\to-)=\alpha^{-1}\circ\beta
.
Moreover, we obtain
f
and its inverse by taking the ‘mate’
4
4

4

The use of the term ‘mate’ in this context is not standard nomenclature, but it gives a good feel for the style of the proofs that follow using mates.
of the appropriate identity morphisms under the natural isomorphisms

{\mathcal{C}}(a\to c)\cong{\mathcal{F}}(c)\cong{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c).

Taking
c=b
, we have
f=\alpha_{b}(\beta^{-1}_{b}(\mathbf{1}_{b}))
.
Taking
c=a
, we have
f^{-1}=\beta_{a}(\alpha_{a}^{-1}(\mathbf{1}_{a}))
.
We summarize this fact as follows:

(2.6)

\textstyle{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{F}}(b)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{C}}(b\to b)}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to a)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{F}}(a)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{C}}(b\to a)}
\textstyle{f\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\beta_{b}^{-1}(\mathbf{1}_{b})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{1}_{b}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{1}_{a}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\alpha_{a}^{-1}(\mathbf{1}_{a})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{f^{-1}}

2.5.
{\mathcal{V}}
-adjunctions

Suppose
{\mathcal{C}}
and
{\mathcal{D}}
are
{\mathcal{V}}
-categories and
{\mathcal{L}}:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{D}}
and
{\mathcal{R}}:{\mathcal{D}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
are
{\mathcal{V}}
-functors.

Definition 2.16
.

We say that
{\mathcal{L}}
is a left
{\mathcal{V}}
-adjoint of
{\mathcal{R}}
(equivalently
{\mathcal{R}}
is a right
{\mathcal{V}}
-adjoint of
{\mathcal{L}}
), denoted
{\mathcal{L}}\dashv_{\mathcal{V}}{\mathcal{R}}
, if
there is a family of isomorphisms
\theta_{a,d}\in{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)\to d)\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to{\mathcal{R}}(d)))
for
a\in{\mathcal{C}}
and
d\in{\mathcal{D}}
,
such that for all
a,b\in{\mathcal{C}}
and all
c,d\in{\mathcal{D}}
, the following two diagrams commute:

(2.7)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b){\mathcal{C}}(b\to{\mathcal{R}}(d))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}(a\to{\mathcal{R}}(d))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)\to{\mathcal{L}}(b)){\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{L}}(b)\to d)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)\to d)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{L}}_{a\to b}\theta_{b,d}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{a,d}^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-}

(2.8)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)\to c){\mathcal{D}}(c\to d)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)\to d)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}(a\to{\mathcal{R}}(c)){\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{R}}(c)\to{\mathcal{R}}(d))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}(a\to{\mathcal{R}}(d))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{a,c}{\mathcal{R}}_{c\to d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\theta_{a,d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-}

Remark 2.17
.

In the above definition, we would like to be able to simply say that there is a
1_{\mathcal{V}}
-graded natural isomorphism between functors

(2.9)

{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)\to d)\cong{\mathcal{C}}(a\to{\mathcal{R}}(d)).

from
{\mathcal{C}}^{\op}\times{\mathcal{D}}\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}

but this doesn’t quite make sense at our level of generality; without assuming
{\mathcal{V}}
is braided we cannot form
{\mathcal{C}}^{\op}
nor the product
{\mathcal{V}}
-category.

Remark 2.18
.

Existence of a left
{\mathcal{V}}
-adjoint of
{\mathcal{R}}
is equivalent to each
{\mathcal{V}}
-functor
{\mathcal{C}}(a\to{\mathcal{R}}(-)):{\mathcal{D}}\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}
being
{\mathcal{V}}
-representable as in Example
2.8
, and similarly for the existence of a right
{\mathcal{V}}
-adjoint
[
Kel05
, §1.11]
.
Here, the
{\mathcal{V}}
-functor
{\mathcal{C}}(a\to{\mathcal{R}}(-))
is defined by setting

{\mathcal{C}}(a\to{\mathcal{R}}(-))_{c\to d}
to be the mate of
(\mathbf{1}{\mathcal{R}}_{c\to d})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)
under the adjunction

{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{D}}(c\to d)\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}({\mathcal{C}}(a\to{\mathcal{R}}(c))\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to{\mathcal{R}}(d)))\cong{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{C}}(a\to{\mathcal{R}}(c)){\mathcal{D}}(c\to d)\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to{\mathcal{R}}(d))).

Indeed, by applying Adjunction (
2.1
), a
{\mathcal{V}}
-natural isomorphism
\theta
as in (
2.9
) is equivalent to a collection of
1_{\mathcal{V}}
-graded
{\mathcal{V}}
-natural isomorphisms
\sigma^{a}:{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)\to-)\Rightarrow{\mathcal{C}}(a\to{\mathcal{R}}(-))
.
One simply sets
\sigma^{a}_{d}
to be the mate of
\theta_{a\to d}
under the adjunction

{\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}({\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)\to d)\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to{\mathcal{R}}(d)))\cong{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)\to d)\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to{\mathcal{R}}(d))).

Remark 2.19
.

Note that if
{\mathcal{L}}\dashv_{\mathcal{V}}{\mathcal{R}}
, then we have an adjunction of underlying functors
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathcal{V}}\dashv{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathcal{V}}

[
Kel05
, §1.11]
.
Indeed the unit and counit of the underlying adjunction, denoted

\eta^{\mathcal{V}}:\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}}\Rightarrow{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathcal{V}}\circ{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathcal{V}}
and
\varepsilon^{\mathcal{V}}:{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathcal{V}}\circ{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathcal{V}}\Rightarrow\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathcal{V}}}
,
are given by

\eta^{\mathcal{V}}_{a}:=j_{{\mathcal{L}}(a)}\circ\theta_{a,{\mathcal{L}}(a)}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\to{\mathcal{R}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)))

and

\varepsilon^{\mathcal{V}}_{d}:=j_{{\mathcal{R}}(d)}\circ\theta^{-1}_{{\mathcal{R}}(d),d}\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{R}}(d))\to d)
.
For later use, we record the helpful relations

(2.10)

\begin{split}\theta_{a,d}&=(\eta^{\mathcal{V}}_{a}{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{L}}(a)\to d})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)\in{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)\to d)\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to{\mathcal{R}}(d)))\\
\kappa_{a,d}:=\theta^{-1}_{a,d}&=({\mathcal{L}}_{a\to{\mathcal{R}}(d)}\varepsilon^{\mathcal{V}}_{d})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-)\in{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{C}}(a\to{\mathcal{R}}(d))\to{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)\to d))\end{split}

which is easily verified using the naturality conditions (
2.7
) and (
2.8
).

Warning 2.20
.

We warn the reader that the notation for the unit
\varepsilon^{\mathcal{V}}
and counit
\eta^{\mathcal{V}}
of the underlying adjunction of an arbitrary
{\mathcal{V}}
-adjunction
{\mathcal{L}}\dashv{\mathcal{R}}
is very similar to the notation for the unit
\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}
and the counit
\eta^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}
of the adjunction

{\mathcal{V}}(uv\to w)\cong{\mathcal{V}}(v\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to w))
.
The unit of this adjunction
\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}
is also called the evaluation morphism
for the self-enrichment
\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}
from Notation
2.1
and Example
2.6
.

We now prove a helpful lemma on lifting underlying adjunctions to
{\mathcal{V}}
-adjunctions which is distilled from the first paragraph in
[
Kel05
, p. 24]
.
Suppose
{\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}}
are
{\mathcal{V}}
-categories with
{\mathcal{V}}
closed, and
{\mathcal{L}}:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{D}}
and
{\mathcal{R}}:{\mathcal{D}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
are
{\mathcal{V}}
-functors.
Suppose
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathcal{V}}\dashv{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathcal{V}}
, and denote the unit and counit of this adjunction by

\eta^{\mathcal{V}}_{a}\in{\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to{\mathcal{R}}({\mathcal{L}}(a))))

and

\varepsilon^{\mathcal{V}}_{d}\in{\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{R}}(d))\to d))
.
For
a\in{\mathcal{C}}
and
d\in{\mathcal{D}}
, define
\theta_{a,d}
and
\kappa_{a,d}
via the formulas (
2.10
) above.
(We write
\kappa
instead of
\theta^{-1}
as we don’t yet know
\theta
is invertible.)

Lemma 2.21
.

If
\kappa_{a,d}=\theta_{a,d}^{-1}
for all
a\in{\mathcal{C}}
and
d\in{\mathcal{D}}
, then
{\mathcal{L}}\dashv_{\mathcal{V}}{\mathcal{R}}
.

Proof.

It remains to prove the naturality conditions (
2.7
) and (
2.8
) for
\theta
.
We prove (
2.8
) and the other is as easy.
For all
a\in{\mathcal{C}}
and
c,d\in{\mathcal{D}}
,

\displaystyle(\theta_{a,c}{\mathcal{R}}_{c\to d})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)

\displaystyle=([(\eta^{\mathcal{V}}_{a}{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{L}}(a)\to c})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)]{\mathcal{R}}_{c\to d})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)

\displaystyle=(\eta^{\mathcal{V}}_{a}[({\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{L}}(a)\to c}{\mathcal{R}}_{c\to d})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)])\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)

\displaystyle=(\eta^{\mathcal{V}}_{a}[(-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-)\circ{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{L}}(a)\to d}])\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)

\displaystyle=(-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-)\circ([\eta^{\mathcal{V}}_{a}{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{L}}(a)\to d}]\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-))

\displaystyle=(-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-)\circ\theta_{a,d}.\qed

In Section
4.1
, we will prove another result, Theorem
4.5
, about promoting ordinary adjunctions to
{\mathcal{V}}
-adjunctions.

2.6.
Tensored
{\mathcal{V}}
-categories

Suppose that
{\mathcal{V}}
is closed so that we may form
\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}
.

Example 2.22
.

We may consider
u\otimes-
as a
{\mathcal{V}}
-functor
\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}
by setting
(u\otimes-)_{v\to w}
to be the mate of
\mathbf{1}_{u}\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{v\to w}
under the adjunction

{\mathcal{V}}(uv\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(v\to w)\to uw)\cong{\mathcal{V}}(\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(v\to w)\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(uv\to uw))={\mathcal{V}}(\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(v\to w)\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(uv\to uw)).

Similarly, we may consider
\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to-)
as a
{\mathcal{V}}
-functor
\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}
by setting
\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to-)_{v\to w}
to be the mate of
-\circ_{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}-
under the adjunction

\displaystyle{\mathcal{V}}(\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to v)\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(v\to w),\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to w))

\displaystyle\cong{\mathcal{V}}(\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(v\to w)\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to v)\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to w)))

\displaystyle={\mathcal{V}}(\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(v\to w)\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to v)\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to w))).

It is straightforward to compute that
u\otimes-
is a left
{\mathcal{V}}
-adjoint to
\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to-)

[
Kel05
, Eq. (1.27)]
.

Definition 2.23
.

Following
[
Lin81
]
, we call a
{\mathcal{V}}
-category
tensored
if each
{\mathcal{V}}
-representable functor
{\mathcal{R}}^{a}={\mathcal{C}}(a\to-):{\mathcal{C}}\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}
as defined in Example
2.8
admits a left
{\mathcal{V}}
-adjoint
{\mathcal{L}}^{a}:\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
.

Remark 2.24
.

We will define the notion of a tensored
{\mathcal{V}}
-functor between tensored
{\mathcal{V}}
-categories in §
4.1
after we establish the bijective correspondence between
{\mathcal{V}}
-categories and
{\mathcal{V}}
-module categories in the next section.
We will then prove in Theorem
4.5
that we may lift an adjunction of underlying functors
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathcal{V}}\dashv{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathcal{V}}
to a
{\mathcal{V}}
-adjunction if and only if
{\mathcal{L}}
is tensored.

3.
Equivalence between
{\mathcal{V}}
-categories and oplax right
{\mathcal{V}}
-modules

3.1.
{\mathcal{V}}
-categories to oplax
{\mathcal{V}}
-modules

Suppose
{\mathcal{C}}
is a
{\mathcal{V}}
-category.

Definition 3.1
.

We call
{\mathcal{C}}

oplax tensored
if for all
a\in{\mathcal{C}}
, the functor
{\mathcal{R}}_{a}:{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{V}}
given by
b\mapsto{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)
has a left adjoint
{\mathcal{L}}_{a}:{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}
.

(Throughout
[
MP17
, §4.1]
we assumed our categories satisfied this property without naming it.)

When
{\mathcal{C}}
is oplax tensored, we can endow
{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}
with the structure of an oplax right
{\mathcal{V}}
-module category.
First, we define
a\vartriangleleft v={\mathcal{L}}_{a}(v)
for
a\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}
and
v\in{\mathcal{V}}
, so that we have an adjunction

(3.1)

{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\vartriangleleft v\to b)={\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{L}}_{a}(v)\to b)\cong{\mathcal{V}}(v\to{\mathcal{R}}_{a}(b))={\mathcal{V}}(v\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)).

Now for a fixed
a\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}
, for all
v\in{\mathcal{V}}
, the unit of the adjunction
{\mathcal{L}}_{a}\dashv{\mathcal{R}}_{a}
is
\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{a,v}\in{\mathcal{V}}(u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to a\vartriangleleft u))
, which is given by the mate of the identity in
{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\vartriangleleft u\to a\vartriangleleft u)
.

Clearly
\vartriangleleft
is functorial in
{\mathcal{V}}
as
a\vartriangleleft v={\mathcal{L}}_{a}(v)
is defined via a functor.
To show it is functorial in
{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}
, we use Adjunction (
3.1
).
First, given
f\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\to b)
, we get a natural tranformation
\theta^{f}:{\mathcal{R}}_{b}\Rightarrow{\mathcal{R}}_{a}
by precomposition with
f
.
We then define for
v\in{\mathcal{V}}
the morphism
f\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{v}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\vartriangleleft v\to b\vartriangleleft v)
to be the mate of

v\xrightarrow{\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{b,v}}{\mathcal{R}}_{b}(b\vartriangleleft v)\xrightarrow{\theta^{f}_{b\vartriangleleft v}}{\mathcal{R}}_{a}(b\vartriangleleft v)

under Adjunction (
3.1
).
It is now straightforward to show that
-\vartriangleleft-:{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}\otimes{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}
is a bifunctor.
Indeed, to verify the exchange relation

(f\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{u})\circ(\mathbf{1}_{b}\vartriangleleft g)=(\mathbf{1}_{b}\vartriangleleft g)\circ(f\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{u})

for
f\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\to b)
and
g\in{\mathcal{V}}(u\to v)
, we take mates under Adjunction (
3.1
) to see

\displaystyle\mate[(f\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{u})\circ(\mathbf{1}_{b}\vartriangleleft g)]

\displaystyle=\mate[(f\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{u})\circ{\mathcal{L}}_{b}(g)]=\mate[f\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{u}]\circ{\mathcal{R}}_{a}{\mathcal{L}}_{b}(g)

\displaystyle=\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{b,u}\circ\theta^{f}_{b\vartriangleleft u}\circ{\mathcal{R}}_{a}{\mathcal{L}}_{b}(g)=\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{b,u}\circ{\mathcal{R}}_{b}{\mathcal{L}}_{b}(g)\circ\theta^{f}_{b\vartriangleleft v}=g\circ\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{b,v}\circ\theta^{f}_{b\vartriangleleft v}

\displaystyle=\mate[{\mathcal{L}}_{a}(g)\circ\mate[\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{b,v}\circ\theta^{f}_{b\vartriangleleft v}]]=\mate[(\mathbf{1}_{a}\vartriangleleft g)\circ(f\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{v})].

We now show that
{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}
is strongly unital.
For
a\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}
, we define the distinguished morphism
\rho_{a}
to be the mate of
j_{a}=\mathbf{1}_{a}
under the adjunction

{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}\to a)\cong{\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to a))={\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\to a).

Lemma 3.2
.

The morphism
\rho_{a}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}\to a)
is an isomorphism with inverse
\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{a,{1_{\mathcal{V}}}}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\to a\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}})
.

Proof.

Setting
v=1_{\mathcal{V}}
in Adjunction (
3.1
), we get a natural isomorphism
{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}\to b)\cong{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\to b)
.
Thus by the Yoneda lemma as in (
2.6
),
a\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}\cong a
, with explicit isomorphism given by the mate of
\mathbf{1}_{a}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\to a)\cong{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}\to a)
, i.e.,
\rho_{a}
.
The inverse is given by the mate of
\mathbf{1}_{a\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}\to a\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}})\cong{\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to a\vartriangleleft v))
, i.e.,
\eta_{a,{1_{\mathcal{V}}}}^{\mathcal{C}}
.
∎

We now define the oplaxitor

\alpha_{a,u,v}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\vartriangleleft uv\to a\vartriangleleft u\vartriangleleft v)\cong{\mathcal{V}}(uv\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to a\vartriangleleft u\vartriangleleft v))

as the mate of

(3.2)

uv\xrightarrow{\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{a,u}\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{a\vartriangleleft u,v}}{\mathcal{C}}(a\to a\vartriangleleft u){\mathcal{C}}(a\vartriangleleft u\to a\vartriangleleft u\vartriangleleft v)\xrightarrow{-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-}{\mathcal{C}}(a\to a\vartriangleleft u\vartriangleleft v).

It is now straightforward to verify by taking mates of appropriate composites that
({\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}},\alpha,\rho)
is a strongly unital oplax right
{\mathcal{V}}
-module category.
We provide the proof that all
\alpha_{c,1_{\mathcal{V}},u}
and
\alpha_{c,u,1_{\mathcal{V}}}
are invertible below.

Lemma 3.3
.

For all
c\in{\mathcal{C}}
and
u\in{\mathcal{V}}
,

\alpha^{-1}_{c,u,1_{\mathcal{V}}}=\rho_{c\vartriangleleft u}

and

\alpha^{-1}_{c,1_{\mathcal{V}},u}=\rho_{c}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{u}
.

Proof.

First, under Adjunction (
3.1
) setting
a=c\vartriangleleft u
,
v=1_{\mathcal{V}}
, and
b=c\vartriangleleft u
,
using Lemma
3.2
, we have that the mate of
\alpha_{c,u,1_{\mathcal{V}}}\circ\rho_{c\vartriangleleft u}
is given by

(\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{c,u}\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{c\vartriangleleft u,1_{\mathcal{V}}}\rho_{c\vartriangleleft u})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)=\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{c,u},

which is exactly the mate of
\mathbf{1}_{c\vartriangleleft u}
.
Second, under Adjunction (
3.1
) setting
a=c\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}
,
v=u
, and
b=c\vartriangleleft u
,
again using Lemma
3.2
, we have that the mate of
\alpha_{c,1_{\mathcal{V}},u}\circ(\rho_{c}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{u})
is given by

(\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{c,1_{\mathcal{V}}}\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{c\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}},u}(\rho_{c}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{u}))\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)=(\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{c,1_{\mathcal{V}}}\rho_{c}\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{c,u})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)=\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{c,u},

which is exactly the mate of
\mathbf{1}_{c\vartriangleleft u}
.
Here, we used the identity

(\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{c\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}},u}(\rho_{c}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{u}))\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)=(\rho_{c}\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{c,u})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-),

which is easily verified as the mate of each side is
\rho_{c}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{u}
under the adjunction.
∎

3.2.
Oplax
{\mathcal{V}}
-modules to
{\mathcal{V}}
-categories

Suppose
{\mathcal{M}}
is an oplax
{\mathcal{V}}
-module category.
Similar to
[
MP17
, §6]
, we assume that for all
a\in{\mathcal{M}}
, the functor
{\mathcal{L}}_{a}:{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{M}}
by
v\mapsto a\vartriangleleft v
has a right adjoint
{\mathcal{R}}_{a}:{\mathcal{M}}\to{\mathcal{V}}
.
We define a
{\mathcal{V}}
-enriched category
{\mathcal{C}}
by

{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)={\mathcal{R}}_{a}(b)
, so that we have an adjunction

(3.3)

{\mathcal{M}}(a\vartriangleleft v\to b)={\mathcal{M}}({\mathcal{L}}_{a}(v)\to b)\cong{\mathcal{V}}(v\to{\mathcal{R}}_{a}(b))={\mathcal{V}}(v\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)).

We have for all
a,b\in{\mathcal{M}}
the evaluation map (counit morphism)
\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{a\to b}\in{\mathcal{M}}(a\vartriangleleft{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)\to b)
given by the mate of the identity in
{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b))
.
We define our identity elements
j_{a}\in{\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to a))
to be the mate of the distinguished morphism

\rho_{a}\in{\mathcal{M}}(a\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}\to a)
.
We define the composition morphism

-\circ_{{\mathcal{C}}}-\in{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{C}}(a\to b){\mathcal{C}}(b\to c)\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to c))\cong{\mathcal{M}}(a\vartriangleleft({\mathcal{C}}(a\to b){\mathcal{C}}(b\to c))\to c)

as the mate of

\displaystyle a\vartriangleleft{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b){\mathcal{C}}(b\to c)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\alpha_{a,{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b),{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c)}}a\vartriangleleft{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)\vartriangleleft{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{a\to b}\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c)}}b\vartriangleleft{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c)

\displaystyle\xrightarrow{\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{b\to c}}c.

It is straightforward to verify that
{\mathcal{C}}
is a
{\mathcal{V}}
-category.
Indeed, the proof is entirely similar to
[
MP17
, §6.1 and 6.2]
.
We provide the proof below that the
j_{a}
are identity elements in
{\mathcal{C}}
only assuming
{\mathcal{M}}
is oplax and not strongly unital.

Lemma 3.4
.

The morphisms
j_{a}
satisfy the identity axioms.

Proof.

We verify that
(j_{a}\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)=\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}
, and the other equation is similar.
The mate of
(j_{a}\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)
under the adjunction

{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b))\cong{\mathcal{M}}(a\vartriangleleft{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)\to b)

is given by

\displaystyle[\mathbf{1}_{a}\vartriangleleft{\mathcal{F}}(j_{a}\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)})]

\displaystyle\circ\alpha_{a,{\mathcal{C}}(a\to a),{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}\circ[\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{a\to a}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}]\circ\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{a\to b}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{a,1_{\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}\circ[\mathbf{1}_{a}\vartriangleleft j_{a}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}]\circ[\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{a\to a}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}]\circ\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{a\to b}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{a,1_{\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}\circ[\rho_{a}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}]\circ\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{a\to b}

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{a\to b}

which is exactly the mate of
\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}
.
In the first equality above, we used naturality of
\alpha
, in the second we used
(\mathbf{1}_{a}\vartriangleleft j_{a})\circ\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{a\to a}=\rho_{a}
(which is proven by taking mates), and in the third we used the unitality axiom for
{\mathcal{M}}
.
∎

3.3.
Equivalence

We now restrict to the setting of oplax
{\mathcal{V}}
-modules which are strongly unital, and prove Theorem
1.4
.
The proof is very close to
[
MP17
, §7]
.

Starting with a strongly unital oplax
{\mathcal{V}}
-module
{\mathcal{M}}
, we can construct the
{\mathcal{V}}
-category
{\mathcal{C}}
as in Section
3.2
, and obtain the strongly unital oplax
{\mathcal{V}}
-module
{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}
as in Section
3.1
.
Since
{\mathcal{C}}
and
{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}
have the same objects as
{\mathcal{M}}
, we can define
{\mathcal{F}}:{\mathcal{M}}\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}
to be the identity on objects.
On morphims, we define
{\mathcal{F}}
by the adjunction

(3.4)

{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\to b)\cong{\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b))\cong{\mathcal{M}}(a\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}\to b)\cong{\mathcal{M}}(a\to b)

This is a functor similar to
[
MP17
, Prop. 6.12]
.
We define
\mu_{m,v}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{F}}(m\vartriangleleft v)\to{\mathcal{F}}(m)\vartriangleleft v)={\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(m\vartriangleleft v\to m\vartriangleleft v)
to be
j_{m\vartriangleleft v}
.
It is easy to check
({\mathcal{F}},\mu)
is a functor of oplax
{\mathcal{V}}
-modules.
Since
{\mathcal{F}}
is the identity on objects,
{\mathcal{F}}
is essentially surjective.
By (
3.4
),
{\mathcal{F}}
is clearly fully faithful.
Hence
({\mathcal{F}},\mu)
is an equivalence of oplax
{\mathcal{V}}
-modules.

Conversely, starting with a
{\mathcal{V}}
-category
{\mathcal{C}}
, we endow
{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}
with the structure of a strongly unital oplax
{\mathcal{V}}
-module as in Section
3.1
, and we obtain a
{\mathcal{V}}
-category
{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
from
{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}
as in Section
3.2
.
We define
{\mathcal{V}}
-functors
{\mathcal{G}}:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
and
{\mathcal{H}}:{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}\to{\mathcal{C}}
as in
[
MP17
, Def. 7.5]
.
On objects,
{\mathcal{G}}(c)={\mathcal{H}}(c)=c
, since
{\mathcal{C}}
,
{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}
, and
{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}
all have the same objects.
We define
{\mathcal{G}}_{a\to b}
and
{\mathcal{H}}_{a\to b}
via adjunction:

\textstyle{{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
(
3.1
)
\textstyle{{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\vartriangleleft{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)\to b)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
(
3.3
)
\textstyle{{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}(a\to b))}
\textstyle{\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{a\to b}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{G}}_{a\to b}}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}(a\to b)\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}(a\to b))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
(
3.3
)
\textstyle{{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\vartriangleleft{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}(a\to b)\to b)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\cong}
(
3.1
)
\textstyle{{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}(a\to b)\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b))}
\textstyle{\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}(a\to b)}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\varepsilon^{{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}}_{a\to b}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{H}}_{a\to b}}

Here, we write
\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}
for the counit of Adjunction (
3.1
), which should not be confused with the counit of Adjunction (
3.3
) from Section
3.2
.
That
{\mathcal{G}}
and
{\mathcal{H}}
are
{\mathcal{V}}
-functors which witness a
{\mathcal{V}}
-equivalence of
{\mathcal{V}}
-categories is identical to the proof of
[
MP17
, Thm. 7.4]
.
Indeed, to see
{\mathcal{G}}
and
{\mathcal{H}}
are mutually inverse, we use the Yoneda Lemma as in (
2.6
).
The adjunctions (
3.1
) and (
3.3
) give us a natural isomorphism between representable functors
{\mathcal{V}}(-\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b))\cong{\mathcal{V}}(-\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}(a\to b))
:

{\mathcal{V}}(v\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b))\underset{\eqref{eq:V-cat to V-mod adjunction}}{\cong}{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\vartriangleleft v\to b)\underset{\eqref{eq:V-mod to V-cat adjunction}}{\cong}{\mathcal{V}}(v\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}(a\to b)).

4.
{\mathcal{V}}
-adjunctions and strong
{\mathcal{V}}
-modules

In this section we assume
{\mathcal{V}}
is closed so that we may form
\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}
.
We now give a condition on a
{\mathcal{V}}
-enriched category
{\mathcal{C}}
which corresponds to
{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}
being a strong right
{\mathcal{V}}
-module category.
We begin by defining the notion of
tensored

{\mathcal{V}}
-functors and use them to prove a helpful result about promoting underlying adjunctions to
{\mathcal{V}}
-adjunctions.

4.1.
Tensored
{\mathcal{V}}
-functors

Throughout this subsection
{\mathcal{C}}
is a
{\mathcal{V}}
-category such that for all
a\in{\mathcal{C}}
, the functor
{\mathcal{R}}_{a}:{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{V}}
given by
b\mapsto{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)
has a left adjoint
{\mathcal{L}}_{a}
so that
{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}
is a strongly unital right oplax
{\mathcal{V}}
-module by Theorem
1.4
, and similarly for
{\mathcal{D}}
.

Lemma 4.1
.

If
{\mathcal{F}}:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{D}}
is a
{\mathcal{V}}
-functor, the underlying functor
{\mathcal{F}}^{\mathcal{V}}:{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathcal{V}}
can be canonically endowed with the structure of a strongly unital lax
{\mathcal{V}}
-module functor by defining,
for
c\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}
and
v\in{\mathcal{V}}
,

\displaystyle\mu_{c,v}\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{F}}(c)\vartriangleleft v\to{\mathcal{F}}(c\vartriangleleft v))

\displaystyle={\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{F}}(c)\vartriangleleft v\to{\mathcal{F}}(c\vartriangleleft v)))

\displaystyle\cong{\mathcal{V}}(v\to{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{F}}(c)\to{\mathcal{F}}(c\vartriangleleft v)))

as the mate of
\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{c,v}\circ{\mathcal{F}}_{c\to c\vartriangleleft v}
, where
\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{c,v}\in{\mathcal{V}}(v\to{\mathcal{C}}(c\to c\vartriangleleft v))\cong{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(c\vartriangleleft v\to c\vartriangleleft v)
is the unit of the adjunction.

Proof.

We first note that for
c\in{\mathcal{C}}
,
\mu_{c,1_{\mathcal{V}}}
is the mate of
{\mathcal{F}}^{\mathcal{V}}(\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{c,1_{\mathcal{V}}})=\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{c,1_{\mathcal{V}}}\circ{\mathcal{F}}_{c\to c\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}}\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{F}}(c)\to{\mathcal{F}}(c)\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}))
.
Thus the mate of
\mu_{c,1_{\mathcal{V}}}\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{\mathcal{V}}(\rho^{\mathcal{C}}_{c})
under adjunction
{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{F}}(c)\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{F}}(c))\cong{\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{F}}(c)\to{\mathcal{F}}(c)))
is given by

\displaystyle[(\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{c,1_{\mathcal{V}}}\circ{\mathcal{F}}_{c\to c\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}})(\rho^{\mathcal{C}}_{c}\circ{\mathcal{F}}_{c\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}\to c})]\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-)

\displaystyle=(\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{c,1_{\mathcal{V}}}\rho^{\mathcal{C}}_{c})\circ({\mathcal{F}}_{c\to c\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}}{\mathcal{F}}_{c\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}\to c})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-)

\displaystyle=(\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{c,1_{\mathcal{V}}}\rho^{\mathcal{C}}_{c})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)\circ{\mathcal{F}}_{c\to c}.

\displaystyle=j_{c}\circ{\mathcal{F}}_{c\to c}

\displaystyle=j_{{\mathcal{F}}(c)}

where we used Lemma
3.2
to conclude
(\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{c,1_{\mathcal{V}}}\rho^{\mathcal{C}}_{c})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)=j_{c}
in the third equality.
Now
j_{{\mathcal{F}}(c)}
is exactly the mate of
\rho^{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathcal{F}}(c)}
under the adjunction, and thus the unital axiom holds.

We now prove the associative condition.
The mate of
\alpha_{{\mathcal{F}}(c),u,v}\circ(\mu_{c,u}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{v})\circ\mu_{c\vartriangleleft u,v}
under the adjunction

{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{F}}(c)\vartriangleleft uv\to{\mathcal{F}}(c\vartriangleleft u\vartriangleleft v))\cong{\mathcal{V}}(uv\to{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{F}}(c)\to{\mathcal{F}}(c\vartriangleleft u\vartriangleleft v)))

is given by

\displaystyle(\eta^{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathcal{F}}(c),u}\eta^{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathcal{F}}(c)\vartriangleleft u,v})

\displaystyle\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-)\circ(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{F}}(c)\to{\mathcal{F}}(c)\vartriangleleft u\vartriangleleft v)}(\mu_{c,u}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{v}))\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-)\circ(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{F}}(c)\to{\mathcal{F}}(c\vartriangleleft u)\vartriangleleft v)}\mu_{c,v})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-)

\displaystyle=(\eta^{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathcal{F}}(c),u}[[\eta^{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathcal{F}}(c)\vartriangleleft u,v}(\mu_{c,u}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{v})]\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-)])\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-)\circ(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{F}}(c)\to{\mathcal{F}}(c\vartriangleleft u)\vartriangleleft v)}\mu_{c,v})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-)

\displaystyle=(\eta^{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathcal{F}}(c),u}[(\mu_{c,u}\eta^{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathcal{F}}(c),v})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-)])\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-)\circ(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{F}}(c)\to{\mathcal{F}}(c\vartriangleleft u)\vartriangleleft v)}\mu_{c,v})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-)

\displaystyle=[(\eta^{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathcal{F}}(c),u}\mu_{c,u})(\eta^{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathcal{F}}(c),v}\mu_{c,v})]\circ[(-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-)(-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-)]\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-)

\displaystyle=[(\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{c,u}\circ{\mathcal{F}}_{c\to c\vartriangleleft u})(\eta^{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathcal{F}}(c),v}{\mathcal{F}}_{c\to c\vartriangleleft v})]\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-)

\displaystyle=(\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{c,u}\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{c,v})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)\circ{\mathcal{F}}_{c\to c\vartriangleleft u\vartriangleleft v}

\displaystyle=\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{c,uv}\circ(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(c\to c\vartriangleleft uv)}\alpha_{c,u,v}^{\mathcal{C}})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)\circ{\mathcal{F}}_{c\to c\vartriangleleft u\vartriangleleft v}

\displaystyle=(\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{c,uv}\circ{\mathcal{F}}_{c\to c\vartriangleleft uv})\circ(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(c\to c\vartriangleleft uv)}(\alpha^{\mathcal{C}}_{c,u,v}\circ{\mathcal{F}}_{c\vartriangleleft uv\to c\vartriangleleft u\vartriangleleft v}))\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)

which is exactly the mate of
\mu_{c,uv}\circ{\mathcal{F}}^{\mathcal{V}}(\alpha_{c,u,v})
under the above adjunction.

To verify
\mu_{a,u}
is natural in
a\in{\mathcal{C}}
and
u\in{\mathcal{V}}
, suppose
f\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\to b)
and
g\in{\mathcal{V}}(u\to v)
.
Then the mate of
\mu_{a,u}\circ{\mathcal{F}}(f\vartriangleleft g)
under the adjunction

{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{F}}(a)\vartriangleleft u\to{\mathcal{F}}(b\vartriangleleft v))\cong{\mathcal{V}}(u\to{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{F}}(a)\to{\mathcal{F}}(b\vartriangleleft v)))

is given by

\displaystyle(\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{a,u}(f\vartriangleleft g))

\displaystyle\circ({\mathcal{F}}_{a\to a\vartriangleleft u}{\mathcal{F}}_{a\vartriangleleft u\to b\vartriangleleft v})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-)

\displaystyle=(\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{a,u}(f\vartriangleleft g))\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)\circ{\mathcal{F}}_{a\to b\vartriangleleft v}

\displaystyle=(f(g\circ\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{b,v}))\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)\circ{\mathcal{F}}_{a\to b\vartriangleleft v}

\displaystyle=(f(g\circ\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{b,v}))\circ({\mathcal{F}}_{a\to b\vartriangleleft v}{\mathcal{F}}_{a\to b\vartriangleleft v})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-)

\displaystyle=[(f\circ{\mathcal{F}}_{a\to b})(g\circ(\eta^{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathcal{F}}(b),v}\mu_{b,v})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-))]\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-)

\displaystyle=(f(g\circ\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{b,v}))\circ({\mathcal{F}}_{a\to b\vartriangleleft v}{\mathcal{F}}_{a\to b\vartriangleleft v})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-)

\displaystyle=((f\circ{\mathcal{F}}_{a\to b})(g\circ\eta^{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathcal{F}}(b),v})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-)\circ(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{D}}(a\to{\mathcal{F}}(b)\vartriangleleft v)}\mu_{b,v})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-)

which is exactly the mate of
({\mathcal{F}}(f)\vartriangleleft g)\circ\mu_{b,v}
under the above adjunction.
∎

Definition 4.2
.

We call a
{\mathcal{V}}
-functor
{\mathcal{F}}:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{D}}

tensored
if the canonical maps
\mu_{c,v}\in{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{F}}(c)\vartriangleleft v\to{\mathcal{F}}(c\vartriangleleft v))
from Lemma
4.1
are isomorphisms for all
c\in{\mathcal{C}}
and
v\in{\mathcal{V}}
.

We now prove a helpful result on lifting underlying adjunctions to
{\mathcal{V}}
-adjunctions.
Suppose
{\mathcal{L}}:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{D}}
and
{\mathcal{R}}:{\mathcal{D}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
are
{\mathcal{V}}
-functors such that
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathcal{V}}\dashv{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathcal{V}}
.
Let
\eta^{\mathcal{V}}:\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}}\Rightarrow{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathcal{V}}\circ{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathcal{V}}
and
\varepsilon^{\mathcal{V}}:{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathcal{V}}\circ{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathcal{V}}\Rightarrow\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathcal{V}}}
be the unit and counit of the underlying adjunction respectively.
Recall that for
a\in{\mathcal{C}}
and
d\in{\mathcal{D}}
, we defined

\theta_{a,d}=(\eta^{\mathcal{V}}_{a}{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{L}}(a)\to d})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)

and

\kappa_{a,d}=({\mathcal{L}}_{a\to{\mathcal{R}}(d)}\varepsilon^{\mathcal{V}}_{d})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-)
.
Lemma
2.21
says that
{\mathcal{L}}\dashv_{\mathcal{V}}{\mathcal{R}}
if
\theta_{a,d}^{-1}=\kappa_{a,d}
for all
a\in{\mathcal{C}}
and
d\in{\mathcal{D}}
.

Lemma 4.3
.

For
a\in{\mathcal{C}}
and
d\in{\mathcal{D}}
,
\kappa_{a,d}
is equal to the mate of
\mu^{\mathcal{L}}_{a,{\mathcal{C}}(a\to{\mathcal{R}}(d))}\circ{\mathcal{L}}(\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{a\to{\mathcal{R}}(d)})\circ\varepsilon^{\mathcal{V}}_{d}
under the adjunction

{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)\vartriangleleft{\mathcal{C}}(a\to{\mathcal{R}}(d))\to d)\cong{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{C}}(a\to{\mathcal{R}}(d))\to{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)\to d)).

Proof.

First, for all
a,b\in{\mathcal{C}}
,

(4.1)

\mu^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{a,{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}\circ{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathcal{V}}(\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{a\to b})=(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{L}}(a)}\vartriangleleft{\mathcal{L}}_{a\to b})\circ\varepsilon^{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathcal{L}}(a)\to{\mathcal{L}}(d)},

since both are the mate of
{\mathcal{L}}_{a\to b}
under the adjunction

{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)\vartriangleleft{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)\to{\mathcal{L}}(b))\cong{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)\to{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)\to{\mathcal{L}}(b))).

Next, by naturality of
\varepsilon^{\mathcal{D}}
, we have

(4.2)

\varepsilon^{\mathcal{D}}_{c\to{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{R}}(d))}\circ\varepsilon^{\mathcal{V}}_{d}=(\mathbf{1}_{c}\vartriangleleft[(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{D}}(c\to{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{R}}(d))}\varepsilon^{\mathcal{V}}_{d})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-)])\circ\varepsilon^{\mathcal{D}}_{c\to d}

Now we have that the mate of
\kappa_{a,d}
under the adjunction is given by

\displaystyle(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{L}}(a)}\vartriangleleft[({\mathcal{L}}_{a\to{\mathcal{R}}(d)}\varepsilon^{\mathcal{V}}_{d})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-)])\circ\varepsilon^{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathcal{L}}(a)\to d}

\displaystyle\underset{\text{\eqref{eq:MateOfKappaProofStep2}}}{=}(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{L}}(a)}\vartriangleleft{\mathcal{L}}_{a\to{\mathcal{R}}(d)})\circ\varepsilon^{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathcal{L}}(a)\to{\mathcal{L}}{\mathcal{R}}(d)}\circ\varepsilon^{\mathcal{V}}_{d}

\displaystyle\underset{\text{\eqref{eq:MateOfKappaProofStep1}}}{=}\mu^{\mathcal{L}}_{a,{\mathcal{C}}(a\to{\mathcal{R}}(d))}\circ{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathcal{V}}(\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{a\to{\mathcal{R}}(d)})\circ\varepsilon^{\mathcal{V}}_{d}.\qed

Lemma 4.4
.

If
{\mathcal{L}}
is tensored, then for
a\in{\mathcal{C}}
and
d\in{\mathcal{D}}
,
\theta_{a,d}
is the mate of
\eta^{\mathcal{V}}_{a\vartriangleleft{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)\to d)}\circ{\mathcal{R}}(\mu^{\mathcal{L}}_{a,{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)\to d)})^{-1}\circ{\mathcal{R}}(\varepsilon^{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathcal{L}}(a)\to d})
under the adjunction

{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\vartriangleleft{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)\to d)\to{\mathcal{R}}(d))\cong{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)\to d)\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to{\mathcal{R}}(d))).

Proof.

Similar to the beginning of the proof of Lemma
4.3
, for all
c,d\in{\mathcal{D}}
,

(4.3)

(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{R}}(c)}\vartriangleleft{\mathcal{R}}_{c\to d})\circ\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathcal{R}}(c)\to{\mathcal{R}}(d)}=\mu^{\mathcal{R}}_{c,{\mathcal{D}}(c\to d)}\circ{\mathcal{R}}(\varepsilon^{\mathcal{D}}_{c\to d}),

since both are the mate of
{\mathcal{R}}_{c\to d}
under the adjunction

{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{R}}(c)\vartriangleleft{\mathcal{D}}(c\to d)\to{\mathcal{R}}(d))\cong{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{D}}(c\to d)\to{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{R}}(c)\to{\mathcal{D}}(d))).

Next, for all
a\in{\mathcal{C}}
and
v\in{\mathcal{V}}
,

(4.4)

(\eta^{\mathcal{V}}_{a}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{v})\circ\mu^{{\mathcal{R}}}_{{\mathcal{L}}(a),v}\circ{\mathcal{R}}(\mu^{\mathcal{L}}_{a,v})=\eta^{\mathcal{V}}_{a\vartriangleleft v}.

Indeed, under the adjunction

{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\vartriangleleft v\to{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{L}}(a\vartriangleleft v))\cong{\mathcal{V}}(v\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to{\mathcal{R}}{\mathcal{L}}(a\vartriangleleft v))),

the mate of the left hand side of (
4.4
) is equal to

\displaystyle[\eta^{\mathcal{V}}_{a}(\eta_{v,{\mathcal{L}}(a)}^{{\mathcal{C}}}\circ{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{L}}(a)\to{\mathcal{L}}(a)\vartriangleleft v})

\displaystyle(\mu^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{a,v}\circ{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{L}}(a)\vartriangleleft v\to{\mathcal{L}}(a\vartriangleleft v)})]\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)

\displaystyle=(\eta^{\mathcal{V}}_{a}[(\eta_{v,{\mathcal{L}}(a)}^{{\mathcal{C}}}\mu^{{\mathcal{L}}}_{a,v})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-)])\circ(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to{\mathcal{R}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)))}{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{L}}(a)\to{\mathcal{L}}(a\vartriangleleft v)})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)

\displaystyle=(\eta^{\mathcal{V}}_{a}[\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{a,v}\circ{\mathcal{L}}_{a\to a\vartriangleleft v}\circ{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{L}}(a)\to{\mathcal{L}}(a\vartriangleleft v)}])\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)

\displaystyle=(\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{a,v}\eta^{\mathcal{V}}_{a\vartriangleleft v})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)

which is exactly the mate of the right hand side of (
4.4
).
Finally, the mate of
\theta_{a,d}=(\eta^{\mathcal{V}}_{a}{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{L}}(a)\to d})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)
under the adjunction is given by

\displaystyle(\mathbf{1}_{a}\vartriangleleft(\eta^{\mathcal{V}}_{a}{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{L}}(a)\to d}))

\displaystyle\circ\alpha^{\mathcal{C}}_{a,{\mathcal{C}}(a\to{\mathcal{R}}({\mathcal{L}}(a))),{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{R}}({\mathcal{L}}(a))\to{\mathcal{R}}(d))}\circ(\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{a\to{\mathcal{R}}({\mathcal{L}}(a))}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{R}}({\mathcal{L}}(a))\to{\mathcal{R}}(d))})\circ\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathcal{R}}({\mathcal{L}}(a))\to{\mathcal{R}}(d)}

\displaystyle\hskip 4.26773pt=(\eta^{\mathcal{V}}_{a}\vartriangleleft{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{L}}(a)\to d})\circ\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{{\mathcal{R}}({\mathcal{L}}(a))\to{\mathcal{R}}(d)}

\displaystyle\underset{\text{\eqref{eq:MateOfThetaProofStep2}}}{=}(\eta^{\mathcal{V}}_{a}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)\to d)})\circ\mu^{\mathcal{R}}_{{\mathcal{L}}(a),{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)\to d)}\circ{\mathcal{R}}(\varepsilon^{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathcal{L}}(a)\to d})

\displaystyle\underset{\text{\eqref{eq:MateOfThetaProofStep1}}}{=}\eta^{\mathcal{V}}_{a\vartriangleleft{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)\to d)}\circ{\mathcal{R}}(\mu^{\mathcal{L}}_{a,{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)\to d)})^{-1}\circ{\mathcal{R}}(\varepsilon^{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathcal{L}}(a)\to d}).\qed

Theorem 4.5
.

We have
{\mathcal{L}}\dashv_{\mathcal{V}}{\mathcal{R}}
if and only if
{\mathcal{L}}
is tensored.

Proof.

Suppose
{\mathcal{L}}\dashv_{\mathcal{V}}{\mathcal{R}}
.
Then for all
a\in{\mathcal{C}}
and
v\in{\mathcal{V}}
, we have the following isomorphisms, which are easily seen to be natural in
d\in{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathcal{V}}
by construction:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{L}}(a\vartriangleleft v)\to d)

\displaystyle\cong{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\vartriangleleft v\to{\mathcal{R}}(d))\cong{\mathcal{V}}(v\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to{\mathcal{R}}(d)))

\displaystyle\cong{\mathcal{V}}(v\to{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)\to d))\cong{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)\vartriangleleft v\to d).

Thus we get a natural isomorphism of representable functors
{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)\vartriangleleft v\to-)\cong{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{L}}(a\vartriangleleft v)\to-)
.
Setting
d={\mathcal{L}}(a\vartriangleleft v)
