, by the Yoneda Lemma as in Equation (
2.6
), the mate of
\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{L}}(a\vartriangleleft v)}
under the above series of isomorphisms gives a canonical isomorphism in
{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)\vartriangleleft v\to{\mathcal{L}}(a\vartriangleleft v))
.
We now see that under the above isomorphisms,
\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{L}}(a\vartriangleleft v)}
transforms as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{L}}(a\vartriangleleft v)}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\eta^{\mathcal{V}}_{a\vartriangleleft v}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow(\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{a,v}\eta^{\mathcal{V}}_{a\vartriangleleft v})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow(\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{a,v}\eta^{\mathcal{V}}_{a\vartriangleleft v})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)\circ{\mathcal{L}}_{a\to{\mathcal{R}}({\mathcal{L}}(a\vartriangleleft v))}\circ(\mathbf{1}\varepsilon^{\mathcal{V}}_{{\mathcal{L}}(a\vartriangleleft v)})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt=[(\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{a,v}\circ{\mathcal{L}}_{a\to a\vartriangleleft v})(({\mathcal{L}}^{\mathcal{V}}(\eta^{\mathcal{V}}_{a\vartriangleleft v}\varepsilon^{\mathcal{V}}_{{\mathcal{L}}(a\vartriangleleft v)}))\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-))]\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-)=\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{a,v}\circ{\mathcal{L}}_{a\to a\vartriangleleft v}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\mu^{\mathcal{L}}_{a,v}.

So
\mu^{\mathcal{L}}_{a,v}
is an isomorphism for all
a\in{\mathcal{C}}
and
v\in{\mathcal{V}}
, and thus
{\mathcal{L}}
is tensored.

Conversely, suppose
{\mathcal{L}}
is tensored.
For
v\in{\mathcal{V}}
,
a\in{\mathcal{C}}
, and
d\in{\mathcal{D}}
, using Adjunction (
3.1
), we get the following isomorphisms, which are natural in
v\in{\mathcal{V}}
by construction:

(4.5)

\begin{split}{\mathcal{V}}(v\to{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)\to d))&\cong{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)\vartriangleleft v\to d)\\
&\cong{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{L}}(a\vartriangleleft v)\to d)\\
&\cong{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\vartriangleleft v\to{\mathcal{R}}(d))\\
&\cong{\mathcal{V}}(v\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to{\mathcal{R}}(d))).\end{split}

Setting
v={\mathcal{C}}(a\to{\mathcal{R}}(d))
, by Lemma
4.3
,
\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to{\mathcal{R}}(d))}
transforms as follows under the above isomorphisms:

\displaystyle\kappa_{a,d}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\mu^{\mathcal{L}}_{a,{\mathcal{C}}(a\to{\mathcal{R}}(d))}\circ{\mathcal{L}}(\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{a\to{\mathcal{R}}(d)})\circ\varepsilon^{\mathcal{V}}_{d}\leftrightarrow{\mathcal{L}}(\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{a\to{\mathcal{R}}(d)})\circ\varepsilon^{\mathcal{V}}_{d}\leftrightarrow\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{a\to{\mathcal{R}}(d)}\leftrightarrow\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to{\mathcal{R}}(d))}.

Likewise, setting
v={\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)\to d)
, by Lemma
4.4
,
\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)\to d)}
transforms as follows under the above isomorphisms:

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)\to d)}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\varepsilon^{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathcal{L}}(a)\to d}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow(\mu^{\mathcal{L}}_{a,{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)\to d)})^{-1}\circ\varepsilon^{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathcal{L}}(a)\to d}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\eta^{\mathcal{V}}_{a\vartriangleleft{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)\to d)}\circ{\mathcal{R}}(\mu^{\mathcal{L}}_{a,{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{L}}(a)\to d)})^{-1}\circ{\mathcal{R}}(\varepsilon^{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathcal{L}}(a)\to d})

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\theta_{a,d}

As Equation (
4.5
) is a string of isomorphisms, we have
\theta_{a,d}=\kappa_{a,d}^{-1}
for all
a\in{\mathcal{C}}
and
d\in{\mathcal{D}}
by the Yoneda Lemma as in Equation (
2.6
).
By Lemma
2.21
, we have
{\mathcal{L}}\dashv_{\mathcal{V}}{\mathcal{R}}
.
∎

Thus Theorem
4.5
gives a necessary and sufficient condition to lift the underlying adjunction to a
{\mathcal{V}}
-adjunction under the assumption that
{\mathcal{C}}
and
{\mathcal{D}}
are oplax tensored.
By strengthening the hypothesis to
{\mathcal{C}}
and
{\mathcal{D}}
being tensored, we obtain the following corollary.

Corollary 4.6
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{C}}
and
{\mathcal{D}}
are tensored and
{\mathcal{L}}:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{D}}
and
{\mathcal{R}}:{\mathcal{D}}\to{\mathcal{L}}
are
{\mathcal{V}}
-functors such that
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathcal{V}}\dashv_{\mathcal{V}}{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathcal{V}}
.
Then
{\mathcal{L}}\dashv_{\mathcal{V}}{\mathcal{R}}
if and only if
{\mathcal{L}}
is tensored.

4.2.
{\mathcal{V}}
-adjunctions to strong
{\mathcal{V}}
-modules

Suppose
{\mathcal{C}}
is a
{\mathcal{V}}
-category.
In Section
3.1
, we looked at the functors
{\mathcal{R}}_{a}:{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{V}}
given by
b\mapsto{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)
.
It is important to note that
{\mathcal{R}}_{a}
can be promoted to a
{\mathcal{V}}
-functor
{\mathcal{R}}^{a}:{\mathcal{C}}\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}
as in Example
2.8
.

We assume now that
{\mathcal{C}}
is tensored, so that each
{\mathcal{R}}^{a}
admits a left
{\mathcal{V}}
-adjoint
{\mathcal{L}}^{a}:\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
.
As before, we set
a\vartriangleleft v={\mathcal{L}}^{a}(v)
so that we have a
{\mathcal{V}}
-adjunction

(4.6)

{\mathcal{C}}(a\vartriangleleft v\to b)={\mathcal{C}}({\mathcal{L}}^{a}(v)\to b)\cong\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(v\to{\mathcal{R}}^{a}(b))=\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(v\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)).

We endow
{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}
with the structure of an oplax right
{\mathcal{V}}
-module as in Section
3.1
.
We claim now that
{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}
is actually a strong
{\mathcal{V}}
-module, i.e., the morphisms
\alpha_{a,u,v}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\vartriangleleft uv\to a\vartriangleleft u\vartriangleleft v)
are isomorphisms.
Note that since
{\mathcal{L}}^{a}\dashv_{\mathcal{V}}{\mathcal{R}}^{a}
, by Corollary
4.6
,
{\mathcal{L}}^{a}:\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
is tensored.

Lemma 4.7
.

Under the identification
\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}^{\mathcal{V}}={\mathcal{V}}
in Example
2.11
, the right
{\mathcal{V}}
-module structure of
{\mathcal{V}}
is given by
u\vartriangleleft v:=uv
.

Proof.

Recall that
u\vartriangleleft v={\mathcal{L}}_{u}(v)
where
{\mathcal{L}}_{u}=(u\otimes-)^{\mathcal{V}}
and
(u\otimes-)
is a left
{\mathcal{V}}
-adjoint of
\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to-)
from Example
2.22
.
Now on objects,
(u\otimes-)^{\mathcal{V}}(v)=uv
, and for a morphism
f\in{\mathcal{V}}(v\to w)
,
(u\otimes f)^{\mathcal{V}}\in{\mathcal{V}}(uv\to uw)
is equal to
\mathbf{1}_{u}f
.
∎

Thus for all
a\in{\mathcal{C}}
and
u,v\in{\mathcal{V}}
,

\mu^{{\mathcal{L}}^{a}}_{u,v}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{L}}^{a}(u)\vartriangleleft v\to{\mathcal{L}}^{a}(u\vartriangleleft v))={\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\vartriangleleft u\vartriangleleft v\to a\vartriangleleft uv)

is an isomorphism.

Proposition 4.8
.

For all
a\in{\mathcal{C}}
and
u,v\in{\mathcal{V}}
,
\alpha_{a,u,v}=(\mu^{{\mathcal{L}}^{a}}_{u,v})^{-1}
.
Since
\mu^{{\mathcal{L}}^{a}}_{u,v}
is an isomorphism, so is
\alpha_{a,u,v}
.

Proof.

For
a,b\in{\mathcal{C}}
and
u,v\in{\mathcal{V}}
, the following isomorphisms are natural in
b\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}
by construction.

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\vartriangleleft u\vartriangleleft v\to b)

\displaystyle\cong{\mathcal{V}}(v\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\vartriangleleft u\to b))

\displaystyle\cong{\mathcal{V}}(v\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)))

\displaystyle\cong{\mathcal{V}}(uv\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b))

\displaystyle\cong{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\vartriangleleft uv\to b)

Setting
b=a\vartriangleleft uv
and transforming
\mathbf{1}_{a\vartriangleleft uv}
under the series of isomorphisms yields

\displaystyle\mu^{{\mathcal{L}}^{a}}_{u,v}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\eta^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{u,v}\circ{\mathcal{L}}^{a}_{u\to uv}=\eta^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{u,v}\circ(\mathbf{1}_{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to uv)}\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{a,uv})\circ(-\circ_{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}-)\circ\kappa_{u,a\vartriangleleft uv}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\eta^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{u,v}\circ(\mathbf{1}_{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to uv)}\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{a,uv})\circ(-\circ_{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}-)

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{a,uv}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\mathbf{1}_{a\vartriangleleft uv}.

Note we used the expression for
\kappa
in terms of
{\mathcal{L}}^{a}
and the unit
\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}
of the underlying adjunction from (
2.10
).
Now setting
b=a\vartriangleleft u\vartriangleleft v
and transforming
\mathbf{1}_{a\vartriangleleft u\vartriangleleft v}
under the series of isomorphisms yields

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{a\vartriangleleft u\vartriangleleft v}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{a\vartriangleleft u,v}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{a\vartriangleleft u,v}\circ\theta_{u,a\vartriangleleft u\vartriangleleft v}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow[\mathbf{1}_{u}(\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{a\vartriangleleft u,v}\circ\theta_{u,a\vartriangleleft u\vartriangleleft v})]\circ\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to a\vartriangleleft u\vartriangleleft v)}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow[\mathbf{1}_{a}\vartriangleleft([\mathbf{1}_{u}(\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{a\vartriangleleft u,v}\circ\theta_{u,a\vartriangleleft u\vartriangleleft v})]\circ\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to a\vartriangleleft u\vartriangleleft v)})]\circ\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{a\to a\vartriangleleft u\vartriangleleft v}

\displaystyle\qquad=[\mathbf{1}_{a}\vartriangleleft([(\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{a,u}\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{a\vartriangleleft u,v})\circ(\mathbf{1}_{u}{\mathcal{R}}_{a\vartriangleleft u\to a\vartriangleleft u\vartriangleleft v})]\circ\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to a\vartriangleleft u)\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to a\vartriangleleft u\vartriangleleft v)})]\circ\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{a\to a\vartriangleleft u\vartriangleleft v}

\displaystyle\qquad=(\mathbf{1}_{a}\vartriangleleft[(\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{a,u}\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{a\vartriangleleft u,v})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)])\circ\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{a\to a\vartriangleleft u\vartriangleleft v}

\displaystyle\qquad=\alpha_{a,u,v}.

Note we used the expression for
\theta
in terms of
{\mathcal{R}}^{a}
and
\eta^{\mathcal{C}}
from (
2.10
), together with Example
2.8
.
We are now finished by the Yoneda Lemma as in (
2.6
).
∎

4.3.
Strong
{\mathcal{V}}
-modules to
{\mathcal{V}}
-adjunctions

Now suppose
{\mathcal{M}}
is a strong right
{\mathcal{V}}
-module category.
As in Section
3.2
, we assume the functors
{\mathcal{L}}_{a}:{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{M}}
given by
v\mapsto m\vartriangleleft v
have right adjoints, and we use the adjunctions
{\mathcal{L}}_{a}\dashv{\mathcal{R}}_{a}
to construct a
{\mathcal{V}}
-enriched category
{\mathcal{C}}
.

We now show that when
{\mathcal{M}}
is strong, the functors
{\mathcal{L}}_{a}:{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{M}}
can be promoted to
{\mathcal{V}}
-functors
{\mathcal{L}}^{a}:\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
.
Indeed, we define
{\mathcal{L}}^{a}_{u\to v}
in

(4.7)

{\mathcal{V}}(\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to v)\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\vartriangleleft u\to a\vartriangleleft v))\cong{\mathcal{M}}(a\vartriangleleft u\vartriangleleft\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to v)\to a\vartriangleleft v)

to be the mate of
\alpha^{-1}_{a,u,\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to v)}\circ(\mathbf{1}_{a}\vartriangleleft\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{u\to v})
, where the existence of
\alpha^{-1}
requires that
{\mathcal{M}}
is strong.
Note that the usual calculations with mates imply

(4.8)

\alpha^{-1}_{a,u,\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to v)}\circ(\mathbf{1}_{a}\vartriangleleft\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{u\to v})=(\mathbf{1}_{a\vartriangleleft u}\vartriangleleft{\mathcal{L}}^{a}_{u\to v})\circ\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{a\vartriangleleft u\to a\vartriangleleft v}.

Lemma 4.9
.

We have
{\mathcal{L}}^{a}
is a
{\mathcal{V}}
-functor.
Under the identification
\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}^{\mathcal{V}}={\mathcal{V}}
from Example
2.11
, the underlying functor of
{\mathcal{L}}^{a}
is
({\mathcal{L}}^{a})^{\mathcal{V}}={\mathcal{L}}_{a}
.

Proof.

We must show that for all
u,v,w\in{\mathcal{V}}
,
(-\circ_{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}-)\circ{\mathcal{L}}^{a}_{u\to w}=({\mathcal{L}}^{a}_{v\to w}{\mathcal{L}}^{a}_{v\to w})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)
.
The mate of
(-\circ_{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}-)\circ{\mathcal{L}}^{a}_{u\to w}
under Adjunction (
4.7
) is given by

\displaystyle(\mathbf{1}_{a\vartriangleleft u}\vartriangleleft(-\circ_{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}-))\circ\alpha^{-1}_{a,u,\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to w)}\circ(\mathbf{1}_{a}\vartriangleleft\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{u\to w})

\displaystyle=\alpha^{-1}_{a,u,\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to v)\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(v\to w)}\circ(\mathbf{1}_{a}\vartriangleleft[\mathbf{1}_{u}(-\circ_{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}-)])\circ(\mathbf{1}_{a}\vartriangleleft\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{u\to w})

\displaystyle=\alpha^{-1}_{a,u,\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to v)\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(v\to w)}\circ(\mathbf{1}_{a}\vartriangleleft(\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{u\to v}\mathbf{1}_{w}))\circ(\mathbf{1}_{a}\vartriangleleft\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{v\to w})

On the other hand, the mate of
({\mathcal{L}}^{a}_{v\to w}{\mathcal{L}}^{a}_{v\to w})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)
under Adjunction (
4.7
) is given by

\displaystyle(\mathbf{1}_{a\vartriangleleft u}

\displaystyle\vartriangleleft({\mathcal{L}}^{a}_{u\to v}{\mathcal{L}}^{a}_{v\to w}))\circ\alpha_{a\vartriangleleft u,{\mathcal{C}}(a\vartriangleleft u\to a\vartriangleleft v),{\mathcal{C}}(a\vartriangleleft v\to a\vartriangleleft w)}\circ(\bar{\varepsilon}^{\mathcal{C}}_{a\vartriangleleft u\to a\vartriangleleft v}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\vartriangleleft v\to a\vartriangleleft w)})\circ\bar{\varepsilon}^{\mathcal{C}}_{a\vartriangleleft v\to a\vartriangleleft w}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{a\vartriangleleft u,\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to v),\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(v\to w)}\circ(\mathbf{1}_{a\vartriangleleft u}\vartriangleleft{\mathcal{L}}^{a}_{u\to v}\vartriangleleft{\mathcal{L}}^{a}_{v\to w})\circ(\bar{\varepsilon}^{\mathcal{C}}_{a\vartriangleleft u\to a\vartriangleleft v}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\vartriangleleft v\to a\vartriangleleft w)})\circ\bar{\varepsilon}^{\mathcal{C}}_{a\vartriangleleft v\to a\vartriangleleft w}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{a\vartriangleleft u,\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to v),\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(v\to w)}\circ(\alpha^{-1}_{a,u,\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to v)}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{w})\circ(\mathbf{1}_{a}\vartriangleleft\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{u\to v}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{w})\circ\alpha^{-1}_{a,v,\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(v\to w)}\circ(\mathbf{1}_{a}\vartriangleleft\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{v\to w})

\displaystyle=\alpha^{-1}_{a,u,\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to v)\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(v\to w)}\circ(\mathbf{1}_{a}\vartriangleleft(\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{u\to v}\mathbf{1}_{w}))\circ(\mathbf{1}_{a}\vartriangleleft\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{v\to w})

The second equality used two instances of (
4.8
), and the last equality used the naturality and associativity of
\alpha
.
∎

Now since we defined
{\mathcal{C}}
by taking the same objects as
{\mathcal{M}}
and setting
{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)={\mathcal{R}}_{a}(b)
, we see that each
{\mathcal{R}}_{a}
can be promoted to a
{\mathcal{V}}
-representable
{\mathcal{V}}
-functor
{\mathcal{R}}^{a}:{\mathcal{C}}\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}
whose underlying functor is
{\mathcal{R}}_{a}
by Lemma
2.14
.

Proposition 4.10
.

For all
a\in{\mathcal{C}}
and
u,v\in{\mathcal{V}}
,
\mu^{{\mathcal{L}}^{a}}_{u,v}=\alpha_{a,u,v}^{-1}
.
Since
\alpha_{a,u,v}
is an isomorphism, so is
\mu^{{\mathcal{L}}^{a}}_{u,v}
.

Proof.

As
\mu^{{\mathcal{L}}^{a}}_{u,v}
is defined by taking mates, we have

\displaystyle\mu^{{\mathcal{L}}^{a}}_{u,v}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{1}_{a\vartriangleleft u}\vartriangleleft\eta^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{u,v}\circ{\mathcal{L}}^{a}_{u\to uv})\circ\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{a\vartriangleleft u\to a\vartriangleleft uv}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{1}_{a\vartriangleleft u}\vartriangleleft\eta^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{u,v})\circ\alpha^{-1}_{a,u,uv}\circ(\mathbf{1}_{a\vartriangleleft u}\vartriangleleft\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{u\to uv})

(by (
4.8
))

\displaystyle=\alpha^{-1}_{a,u,v}\circ(\mathbf{1}_{a\vartriangleleft u}\vartriangleleft\eta^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{u,v})\circ(\mathbf{1}_{a}\vartriangleleft\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{u\to uv})

(by naturality)

\displaystyle=\alpha^{-1}_{a,u,v}.

Corollary 4.11
.

The
{\mathcal{V}}
-functor
{\mathcal{L}}^{a}
is a left
{\mathcal{V}}
-adjoint for
{\mathcal{R}}^{a}
.
Thus
{\mathcal{C}}
is tensored.

Proof.

Since
{\mathcal{L}}_{a}\dashv{\mathcal{R}}_{a}
for all
a\in{\mathcal{C}}
, by Theorem
4.5
, it suffices to prove that each
{\mathcal{L}}^{a}
is tensored.
This is exactly the content of Proposition
4.10
.
∎

5.
Completion for
{\mathcal{V}}
-categories

For this section, we assume
{\mathcal{V}}
is closed so that we may form
\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}
, and
{\mathcal{C}}
is oplax tensored.

5.1.
The completion operation

Suppose
{\mathcal{C}}
is a
{\mathcal{V}}
-category.

Definition 5.1
.

We define the completion
\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}
to be the
{\mathcal{V}}
-category whose objects are of the form
a\blacktriangleleft u
where
a\in{\mathcal{C}}
and
u\in{\mathcal{V}}
,
and whose hom objects are given by
\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}(a\blacktriangleleft u\to b\blacktriangleleft v)=\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)v)
.
The identity element
j_{a\blacktriangleleft u}
is the mate of
j_{a}\mathbf{1}_{u}
under the adjunction

{\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}(a\blacktriangleleft u\to a\blacktriangleleft u))={\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to a)u))\cong{\mathcal{V}}(u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to a)u).

The composition morphism
-\circ_{\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}}-
is the mate of

(\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)v}\mathbf{1}_{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(v\to{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c)w)})\circ(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{v\to{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c)w})\circ((-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)\mathbf{1}_{w})

under the adjunction

\displaystyle{\mathcal{V}}(\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}(a\blacktriangleleft u\to b\blacktriangleleft v)

\displaystyle\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}(b\blacktriangleleft v\to c\blacktriangleleft w)\to\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}(a\blacktriangleleft u\to c\blacktriangleleft w))

\displaystyle={\mathcal{V}}(\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)v)\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(v\to{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c)w)\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to c)w))

\displaystyle\cong{\mathcal{V}}(u\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)v)\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(v\to{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c)w)\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to c)w)

It is straightforward to verify that
\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is a
{\mathcal{V}}
-category.

Lemma 5.2
.

For every
a\blacktriangleleft u\in\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}
, there is a canonical
{\mathcal{V}}
-functor
{\mathcal{L}}^{a\blacktriangleleft u}=a\blacktriangleleft u-:\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}\to\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}
.

Proof.

On objects, we define
{\mathcal{L}}^{a\blacktriangleleft u}(v)=a\blacktriangleleft uv
.
For
v,w\in{\mathcal{V}}
, we define
{\mathcal{L}}^{a\blacktriangleleft u}_{v\to w}
to be the mate of
j_{a}\mathbf{1}_{u}\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{v\to w}
under the adjunction

\displaystyle{\mathcal{V}}(\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(v\to w)\to\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}(a\blacktriangleleft uv\to a\blacktriangleleft uw))

\displaystyle={\mathcal{V}}(\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(v\to w)\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(uv\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to a)uw))

\displaystyle\cong{\mathcal{V}}(uv\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(v\to w)\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to a)uw).

To verify that
{\mathcal{L}}^{a\blacktriangleleft u}
is a
{\mathcal{V}}
-functor, we see that the mate of
({\mathcal{L}}^{a\blacktriangleleft u}_{v\to w}{\mathcal{L}}^{a\blacktriangleleft u}_{w\to x})\circ(-\circ_{\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}}-)
is given by

\displaystyle(\mathbf{1}_{uv}{\mathcal{L}}^{a\blacktriangleleft u}_{v\to w}{\mathcal{L}}^{a\blacktriangleleft u}_{w\to x})

\displaystyle\circ(\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{uv\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to a)uw}\mathbf{1}_{[uw,{\mathcal{C}}(a\to a)ux]})\circ(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to a)}\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{uw\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to a)ux})\circ((-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)\mathbf{1}_{ux})

\displaystyle=[j_{a}\mathbf{1}_{u}][(\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{v\to w}\mathbf{1}_{x})\circ\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{w\to x}]

\displaystyle=[j_{a}\mathbf{1}_{u}][(-\circ_{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}-)\circ\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{v\to x}]

which is exactly the mate of
(-\circ_{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}-)\circ{\mathcal{L}}^{a\blacktriangleleft u}_{v\to x}
.
∎

We define the underlying functor
{\mathcal{L}}_{a\blacktriangleleft u}
on objects by
{\mathcal{L}}_{a\blacktriangleleft u}(v)=a\blacktriangleleft uv
,
and on the morphism
f\in{\mathcal{V}}(v\to w)
, we have that
{\mathcal{L}}_{a\blacktriangleleft u}(f)
is the mate of
j_{a}\mathbf{1}_{u}f\in{\mathcal{V}}(uv\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to a)uw)
under the adjunction.

Proposition 5.3
.

The underlying functor
{\mathcal{L}}_{a\blacktriangleleft u}
is left adjoint to the underlying functor
{\mathcal{R}}_{a\blacktriangleleft u}
.

Proof.

Notice we have a series of isomorphisms which are clearly natural in
v\in{\mathcal{V}}
:

(5.1)

\begin{split}\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}^{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{L}}_{a\blacktriangleleft u}(v)\to b\blacktriangleleft w)&={\mathcal{V}}(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{V}}}\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(uv\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)w))\\
&\cong{\mathcal{V}}(uv\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)w)\\
&\cong{\mathcal{V}}(v\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)w))\\
&={\mathcal{V}}(v\to{\mathcal{R}}_{a\blacktriangleleft u}(b\blacktriangleleft w)).\end{split}

It remains to show the above isomorphisms are natural in
b\blacktriangleleft w\in\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}^{\mathcal{V}}
.
We must show that for every

f\in\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}^{\mathcal{V}}(b\blacktriangleleft w\to c\blacktriangleleft x)={\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to[w,{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c)x])
,
the following diagram commutes:

\textstyle{\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\blacktriangleleft uv\to b\blacktriangleleft w)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\scriptstyle{-\circ f}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{V}}(v\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)w)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{-\circ{\mathcal{R}}_{a\blacktriangleleft u}(f)}
\textstyle{\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\blacktriangleleft uv\to c\blacktriangleleft x)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\psi}
\textstyle{{\mathcal{V}}(v\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to c)x)}

where the horizontal arrows
\psi
are instances of the series of isomorphisms (
5.1
).
It is easiest to do so by taking mates under the adjunction

(5.2)

{\mathcal{V}}(v\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to c)x))\cong{\mathcal{V}}(uv\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to c)x)

which effectively undoes one step of (
5.1
).
Let

g\in\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\blacktriangleleft uv\to b\blacktriangleleft w)={\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(uv\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)w))
, and note that the mate of
\psi(g)
under (
5.2
) with
c=b
and
x=w
is given by

(\mathbf{1}_{uv}g)\circ\varepsilon_{uv\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)w}
.
Now since

{\mathcal{R}}_{a\blacktriangleleft u}(f)\in{\mathcal{V}}(\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}(a\blacktriangleleft u\to b\blacktriangleleft w)\to\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}(a\blacktriangleleft u\to c\blacktriangleleft x))={\mathcal{V}}(\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)w)\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to c)x))

is
(\mathbf{1}_{\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}(a\blacktriangleleft u\to b\blacktriangleleft w)}f)\circ(-\circ_{\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}}-)
,
the mate of
\psi(g)\circ{\mathcal{R}}_{a\blacktriangleleft u}(f)
under (
5.2
) is given by

\displaystyle(\mate(\psi(g))f)

\displaystyle\circ(-\circ_{\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}}-)

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{1}_{u}\mate(\psi(g))f)\circ(\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)w}\mathbf{1}_{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(w\to{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c)x)})\circ(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{w\to{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c)x})\circ((-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)\mathbf{1}_{x})

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{1}_{uv}gf)\circ(\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{uv\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)w}\mathbf{1}_{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(w\to{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c)x)})\circ(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{w\to{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c)x})\circ((-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)\mathbf{1}_{x})

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{1}_{uv}gf)\circ(\mathbf{1}_{uv}(-\circ_{\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}}-))\circ\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{uv\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to c)x},

which is exactly the mate of
\psi(g\circ f)
under Adjunction (
5.2
).
∎

Corollary 5.4
.

The completion
\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is tensored.

Proof.

By Theorem
1.5
, it suffices to show that the stictly unital oplax
{\mathcal{V}}
-module structure on
\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}^{\mathcal{V}}
induced by
a\blacktriangleleft u\vartriangleleft v:={\mathcal{L}}_{a\blacktriangleleft u}(v)=a\blacktriangleleft uv
is strong, which is immediate.
∎

5.2.
Universal property of completion

We now show that completion satisfies a universal property.

Definition 5.5
.

Given a
{\mathcal{V}}
-category
{\mathcal{C}}
(not necessarily tensored), the inclusion
{\mathcal{V}}
-functor
{\mathcal{I}}:{\mathcal{C}}\to\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is given on objects by

a\mapsto a\blacktriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}
and
{\mathcal{I}}_{a\to b}
is the mate of
\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}
under the adjunction

\displaystyle{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)\to\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}(a\blacktriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}\to b\blacktriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}))

\displaystyle={\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)1_{\mathcal{V}}))

\displaystyle\cong{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)).

It is straightforward to verify by taking mates under the above adjunction that
{\mathcal{I}}
is a
{\mathcal{V}}
-functor.
(The key relation is
(\mathbf{1}_{1_{\mathcal{V}}}{\mathcal{I}}_{a\to b})\circ\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)1_{\mathcal{V}}}=\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}
.)

Remark 5.6
.

Recall that for
a\in{\mathcal{C}}
and
u\in{\mathcal{V}}
,

\mu^{\mathcal{I}}_{a,u}
is defined as the mate of
\eta_{u}\circ{\mathcal{I}}_{a\to a\vartriangleleft u}
under the adjunction

\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\blacktriangleleft u\to a\vartriangleleft u\blacktriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}})\cong{\mathcal{V}}(u\to\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}(a\to a\vartriangleleft u\blacktriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}})).

We get the following two identities for
\mu^{\mathcal{I}}_{a,u}
and
\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{a,u}
depending on whether we pass through the second equality below under the identification
\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}^{\mathcal{V}}={\mathcal{V}}
:

\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\blacktriangleleft u\to a\vartriangleleft u\blacktriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}})={\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to a\vartriangleleft u)))={\mathcal{V}}(u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to a\vartriangleleft u)).

Just passing through the first equality, we get the first identity below, and passing to the second, we get the second identity.

(5.3)

\displaystyle\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{a,u}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{1}_{u}\mu^{\mathcal{I}}_{a,u})\circ\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to a\vartriangleleft u)}

(5.4)

\displaystyle\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{a,u}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{1}_{1_{\mathcal{V}}}(\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{a,u}\circ{\mathcal{I}}_{a\to a\vartriangleleft u}))\circ\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to a\vartriangleleft u)1_{\mathcal{V}}}=\mate(\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{a,u}\circ{\mathcal{I}}_{a\to a\vartriangleleft u})=\mu^{\mathcal{I}}_{a,u}

Proposition 5.7
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}}
are
{\mathcal{V}}
-categories with
{\mathcal{D}}
tensored and
{\mathcal{F}}:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{D}}
is a
{\mathcal{V}}
-functor.
There exists a tensored
{\mathcal{V}}
-functor
\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}:\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}\to{\mathcal{D}}

such that
{\mathcal{I}}\circ\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}\cong{\mathcal{F}}
as
{\mathcal{V}}
-functors.

Proof.

We define a
{\mathcal{V}}
-functor
\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}:\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}\to{\mathcal{D}}
by
\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}(a\blacktriangleleft u)={\mathcal{F}}(a)\vartriangleleft u
, and we define
\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}_{a\blacktriangleleft u\to b\blacktriangleleft v}
to be the mate of

\alpha^{-1}_{{\mathcal{F}}(a),u,\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)v)}\circ(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{F}}(a)}\vartriangleleft\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)v})\circ(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{F}}(a)}\vartriangleleft{\mathcal{F}}_{a\to b}\mathbf{1}_{v})\circ\alpha_{{\mathcal{F}}(a),{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{F}}(a)\to{\mathcal{F}}(b)),v}\circ(\varepsilon^{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathcal{F}}(a)\to{\mathcal{F}}(b)}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{v})

under the adjunction

\displaystyle{\mathcal{V}}(\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}(a\blacktriangleleft u\to b\blacktriangleleft v)

\displaystyle\to{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{F}}(a)\vartriangleleft u\to{\mathcal{F}}(b)\vartriangleleft v))

\displaystyle={\mathcal{V}}(\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)v)\to{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{F}}(a)\vartriangleleft u\to{\mathcal{F}}(b)\vartriangleleft v))

\displaystyle\cong{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{F}}(a)\vartriangleleft u\vartriangleleft\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)v)\to{\mathcal{F}}(b)\vartriangleleft v).

To verify that
\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}
is a
{\mathcal{V}}
-functor, we show that the mates of

(\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}_{a\blacktriangleleft u\to b\blacktriangleleft v}\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}_{b\blacktriangleleft v\to c\blacktriangleleft w})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-)

and

(-\circ_{\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}}-)\circ\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}_{a\blacktriangleleft u\to c\blacktriangleleft w}

agree under the above adjunction.
We leave this tedious and straightforward calculation to the reader.

Now for
a\blacktriangleleft u\in\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}
and
v\in{\mathcal{V}}
, we have
\mu^{\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}}_{a\blacktriangleleft u,v}
is the mate of
\eta^{\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}}_{a\blacktriangleleft u,v}\circ\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}_{a\blacktriangleleft u\to a\blacktriangleleft uv}
under the adjunction

\displaystyle{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{F}}(a)\vartriangleleft u\vartriangleleft v\to{\mathcal{F}}(a)\vartriangleleft uv)

\displaystyle={\mathcal{D}}^{\mathcal{V}}(\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}(a\blacktriangleleft u)\vartriangleleft v\to\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}(a\blacktriangleleft uv))

\displaystyle\cong{\mathcal{V}}(v\to{\mathcal{D}}(\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}(a\blacktriangleleft u)\to\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}(a\blacktriangleleft uv))).

Thus we have

\displaystyle\mu^{\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}}_{a\blacktriangleleft u,v}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{1}_{\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}(a\blacktriangleleft u)}\vartriangleleft(\eta^{\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}}_{a\blacktriangleleft u,v}\circ\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}_{a\blacktriangleleft u\to a\blacktriangleleft uv}))\circ\varepsilon^{\mathcal{D}}_{\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}(a\blacktriangleleft u)\to\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}(a\blacktriangleleft uv)}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{F}}(a)\vartriangleleft u}\vartriangleleft\eta^{\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}}_{a\blacktriangleleft u,v})\circ\mate(\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}_{a\blacktriangleleft u\to a\blacktriangleleft uv})

\displaystyle=\alpha^{-1}_{{\mathcal{F}}(a),u,v}\circ(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{F}}(a)}\vartriangleleft[(\mathbf{1}_{u}\eta^{\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}}_{a\blacktriangleleft u,v})\circ\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to a)uv}\circ({\mathcal{F}}_{a\to a}\mathbf{1}_{uv})])\circ\alpha_{{\mathcal{F}}(a),{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{F}}(a)\to{\mathcal{F}}(a)),uv}\circ(\varepsilon^{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathcal{F}}(a)\to{\mathcal{F}}(a)}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{uv})

\displaystyle=\alpha^{-1}_{{\mathcal{F}}(a),u,v}\circ(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{F}}(a)}\vartriangleleft[(j_{a}\circ{\mathcal{F}}_{a\to a})\mathbf{1}_{uv}])\circ\alpha_{{\mathcal{F}}(a),{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{F}}(a)\to{\mathcal{F}}(a)),uv}\circ(\varepsilon^{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathcal{F}}(a)\to{\mathcal{F}}(a)}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{uv})

\displaystyle=\alpha^{-1}_{{\mathcal{F}}(a),u,v}\circ([(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{F}}(a)}\vartriangleleft j_{{\mathcal{F}}(a)})\circ\varepsilon^{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathcal{F}}(a)\to{\mathcal{F}}(a)}]\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{uv})

\displaystyle=\alpha_{{\mathcal{F}}(a),u,v}^{-1},

and so
\mu^{\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}}_{a\blacktriangleleft u,v}
is invertible.
Hence
\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}
is tensored.

We define
\sigma:{\mathcal{F}}\Rightarrow{\mathcal{I}}\circ\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}
by
\sigma_{a}=\eta^{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathcal{F}}(a),1_{\mathcal{V}}}\in{\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{F}}(a)\to{\mathcal{F}}(a)\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}))
, and we note that
\sigma_{a}^{-1}=\rho_{a}^{{\mathcal{D}}}
by Lemma
3.2
.
To verify
\sigma
is a
1_{\mathcal{V}}
-graded
{\mathcal{V}}
-natural isomorphism, it remains to verify (
2.2
).
Notice that (
2.2
) is equivalent to

(\sigma_{a}^{-1}{\mathcal{F}}_{a\to b})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-)=(({\mathcal{I}}\circ\overline{{\mathcal{F}}})_{a\to b}\sigma_{b}^{-1})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-)
.
Under the adjunction

{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)\to{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{F}}(a)\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{F}}(b)))\cong{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{F}}(a)\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}\vartriangleleft{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)\to{\mathcal{F}}(b)),

the mate of
(({\mathcal{I}}\circ\overline{{\mathcal{F}}})_{a\to b}\sigma_{b}^{-1})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-)
is given by

\displaystyle(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{F}}(a)\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}}\vartriangleleft

\displaystyle({\mathcal{I}}\circ\overline{{\mathcal{F}}})_{a\to b}\sigma_{b}^{-1})\circ\alpha_{{\mathcal{F}}(a)\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{F}}(a)\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{F}}(b)\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}),{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{F}}(b)\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{F}}(b))}

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\circ(\varepsilon^{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathcal{F}}(a)\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{F}}(b)\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{F}}(b)\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{F}}(b))})\circ\varepsilon_{{\mathcal{F}}(b)\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{F}}(b)}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{F}}(a)\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}}\vartriangleleft({\mathcal{I}}\circ\overline{{\mathcal{F}}})_{a\to b})\circ\varepsilon^{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathcal{F}}(a)\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{F}}(b)\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}}\circ\rho^{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathcal{F}}(b)}

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{F}}(a)\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}}\vartriangleleft{\mathcal{I}}_{a\to b})\circ\alpha^{-1}_{{\mathcal{F}}(a),1_{\mathcal{V}},\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b))}\circ(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{F}}(a)}\vartriangleleft\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)})

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt\circ(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{F}}(a)}\vartriangleleft{\mathcal{F}}_{a\to b}\mathbf{1}_{1_{\mathcal{V}}})\circ\alpha_{{\mathcal{F}}(a),{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{F}}(a)\to{\mathcal{F}}(b)),1_{\mathcal{V}}}\circ(\varepsilon^{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathcal{F}}(a)\to{\mathcal{F}}(b)}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{1_{\mathcal{V}}})\circ\rho^{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathcal{F}}(b)}

\displaystyle=(\rho^{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathcal{F}}(a)}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)})\circ(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{F}}(a)}\vartriangleleft{\mathcal{F}}_{a\to b})\circ(\rho^{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathcal{F}}(a)\vartriangleleft{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{F}}(a)\to{\mathcal{F}}(b))})^{-1}\circ(\varepsilon^{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathcal{F}}(a)\to{\mathcal{F}}(b)}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{1_{\mathcal{V}}})\circ\rho^{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathcal{F}}(b)}

\displaystyle=(\rho_{{\mathcal{F}}(a)}^{\mathcal{D}}\vartriangleleft{\mathcal{F}}_{a\to b})\circ\varepsilon^{\mathcal{D}}_{{\mathcal{F}}(a)\to{\mathcal{F}}(b)},

which is exactly the mate of
(\sigma_{a}^{-1}{\mathcal{F}}_{a\to b})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{D}}-)
.
Notice here we have used the identities

\alpha^{-1}_{d,v,1_{\mathcal{V}}}=\rho^{\mathcal{D}}_{d\vartriangleleft v}

and

\alpha^{-1}_{d,1_{\mathcal{V}},v}=\rho^{\mathcal{D}}_{d}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{v}

for all
d\in{\mathcal{D}}
and
v\in{\mathcal{V}}
from Lemma
3.3
.
∎

Remark 5.8
.

The above proof raises the following two interesting questions.

(1)

Given a tensored
{\mathcal{V}}
-functor
{\mathcal{G}}:\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}\to{\mathcal{D}}
such that
{\mathcal{I}}\circ{\mathcal{G}}\cong{\mathcal{F}}
, when do we have that
{\mathcal{G}}
is
{\mathcal{V}}
-equivalent to
\overline{{\mathcal{F}}}
?
We are unable to provide any candidate natural isomorphism at this time.

(2)

When
{\mathcal{C}}
is tensored,
setting
{\mathcal{D}}={\mathcal{C}}
and
{\mathcal{F}}=\mathbf{1}^{\mathcal{C}}
, we get a canonical tensored
{\mathcal{V}}
-functor
\overline{\mathbf{1}^{\mathcal{C}}}:\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
such that
\mathbf{1}_{\mathcal{C}}\cong{\mathcal{I}}\circ\overline{\mathbf{1}^{\mathcal{C}}}
.
We show in Lemma
5.9
below that
\tau_{a\blacktriangleleft u}:=\mu^{\mathcal{I}}_{a,u}
defines a
1_{\mathcal{V}}
-graded natural transformation
\tau:\mathbf{1}^{\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}}\Rightarrow\overline{\mathbf{1}^{\mathcal{C}}}\circ{\mathcal{I}}
.
However we do not know how to show that
\overline{\mathbf{1}^{\mathcal{C}}}\circ{\mathcal{I}}
is naturally isomorphic to
\mathbf{1}^{\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}}
, since it may be the case that
\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is too big; we cannot yet identify
a\vartriangleleft u\blacktriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}
and
a\blacktriangleleft u
.
We will solve this problem in the next section by adding additional hypotheses on
{\mathcal{C}}
.

Lemma 5.9
.

Setting
\tau_{a\blacktriangleleft u}:=\mu^{\mathcal{I}}_{a,u}
in

\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\blacktriangleleft u\to a\vartriangleleft u\blacktriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}})=\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}^{\mathcal{V}}(\mathbf{1}^{\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}}(a\blacktriangleleft u)\to{\mathcal{I}}(\overline{\mathbf{1}}^{\mathcal{C}}(a\blacktriangleleft u)))

defines a
1_{\mathcal{V}}
-graded natural transformation
\tau:\mathbf{1}^{\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}}\Rightarrow\overline{\mathbf{1}^{\mathcal{C}}}\circ{\mathcal{I}}
.

Proof.

To show the naturality condition (
2.2
),
we compute mates under the adjunction

\displaystyle{\mathcal{V}}(\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}(a\blacktriangleleft u\to b\blacktriangleleft v)\to\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}(a\blacktriangleleft u\to b\vartriangleleft v\blacktriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}))

\displaystyle={\mathcal{V}}(\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)v)\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b\vartriangleleft v)))

\displaystyle\cong{\mathcal{V}}(u\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)v)\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b\vartriangleleft v)).

Indeed, the mate of
of
(\tau_{a\blacktriangleleft u}(\overline{\mathbf{1}^{\mathcal{C}}}\circ{\mathcal{I}})_{a\blacktriangleleft u\to b\blacktriangleleft v})\circ(-\circ_{\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}}-)

is given by

\displaystyle([(\mathbf{1}_{u}\tau_{a\blacktriangleleft u})\circ\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to a\vartriangleleft u)}]

\displaystyle(\overline{\mathbf{1}^{\mathcal{C}}}\circ{\mathcal{I}})_{a\blacktriangleleft u\to b\blacktriangleleft v}))\circ(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to a\vartriangleleft u)}\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\vartriangleleft u\to b\vartriangleleft v)})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)

\displaystyle\underset{\text{\eqref{eq:MateOfMu=Eta}}}{=}(\eta_{u,a}^{\mathcal{C}}\overline{\mathbf{1}^{\mathcal{C}}}_{a\blacktriangleleft u\to b\blacktriangleleft v})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)

\displaystyle=\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)v}\circ(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{b,v})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)

\displaystyle\underset{\text{\eqref{eq:MateOfMu=Eta}}}{=}\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)v}\circ(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}[(\mathbf{1}_{v}\tau_{b\blacktriangleleft v})\circ\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{v\to{\mathcal{C}}(b\to\vartriangleleft v)}])\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)

which is exactly the mate of
(\mathbf{1}^{\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}}_{a\blacktriangleleft u\to b\blacktriangleleft v}\tau_{b\blacktriangleleft v})\circ(-\circ_{\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}}-)
.
∎

5.3.
When representable
{\mathcal{V}}
-functors are tensored

Recall
{\mathcal{V}}
is closed so we may form
\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}
.
We begin with a lemma that we could have proved in Section
4.1
.

Lemma 5.10
.

Fix
a\in{\mathcal{C}}
, and consider the
{\mathcal{V}}
-functor
{\mathcal{C}}(a\to-):{\mathcal{C}}\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}
.

(1)

Writing
\mu_{b,v}=\mu^{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to-)}_{b,v}
for
b\in{\mathcal{C}}
and
v\in{\mathcal{V}}
, we have

\mu_{b,v}=(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{b,v})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)
.

(2)

For all
g\in{\mathcal{V}}(v\to{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c)w)
,
\mu
satisfies the naturality condition

(5.5)

[\mu_{b,v}(\mathbf{1}_{b}\vartriangleleft g)\alpha^{\mathcal{C}}_{b,{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c),w}(\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{b\to c}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{w})]\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)=(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}g)\circ((-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)\mathbf{1}_{w})\circ\mu_{c,w}.

(Recall the
{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}
-morphisms
\mathbf{1}_{b}\vartriangleleft g
and
\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{b\to c}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{w}
are
{\mathcal{V}}
-morphisms originating at
1_{\mathcal{V}}
.)

Proof.

To prove (1), recall that
we have identified
\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}^{\mathcal{V}}={\mathcal{V}}
, and thus
\mu_{b,v}
is the mate of

\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{b,v}\circ{\mathcal{C}}(a\to-)_{b\to b\vartriangleleft v}
under the adjunction

{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)v\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b\vartriangleleft v))\cong{\mathcal{V}}(v\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}({\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b\vartriangleleft v))).

But the mate of
\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{b,v}\circ{\mathcal{C}}(a\to-)_{b\to b\vartriangleleft v}
is exactly
(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{b,v})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)
under the above adjunction by the definition of
{\mathcal{C}}(a\to-)_{b\to b\vartriangleleft v}
from Example
2.8
.

We prove (2) using the identity from (1) twice.
Note that
\mathbf{1}_{b}\vartriangleleft g:={\mathcal{L}}_{b}(g)
and
\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{b\to c}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{w}
is defined via taking mates as in the beginning of Section
3.1
.
Hence the left hand side of (
5.5
) is equal to

\displaystyle[\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{b,v}

\displaystyle(\mathbf{1}_{b}\vartriangleleft g)\alpha^{\mathcal{C}}_{b,{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c),w}(\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{b\to c}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{w})]\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}[(\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{b,v}{\mathcal{L}}_{b}(g))\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)]\alpha^{\mathcal{C}}_{b,{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c),w}(\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{b\to c}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{w})]\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)

\displaystyle=[\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}(g\circ\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{b,{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c)w})\alpha^{\mathcal{C}}_{b,{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c),w}(\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{b\to c}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{w})]\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)

\displaystyle=[\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}[((g\circ\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{b,{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c)w})\alpha^{\mathcal{C}}_{b,{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c),w})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)])(\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{b\to c}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{w})]\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)

\displaystyle=[\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}[g\circ(\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{b,{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c)}\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{b\vartriangleleft{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c),w})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)](\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{b\to c}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{w})]\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}[g\circ([(\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{b,{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c)}\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{b\to c})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)]\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{c,w})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)])\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)

\displaystyle=[\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}(g\circ(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c)}\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{c,w})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-))]\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}g)\circ((-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)\mathbf{1}_{w})\circ(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to c)}\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{c,w})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-),

which is exactly the right hand side of (
5.5
).
∎

Proposition 5.11
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{C}}
is tensored and
a\in{\mathcal{C}}
and
u\in{\mathcal{V}}
such that the
{\mathcal{V}}
-representable functors
{\mathcal{C}}(a\to-)
and
{\mathcal{C}}(a\vartriangleleft u\to-)
are tensored.
Then we have a natural isomorphism of representable functors

\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\vartriangleleft u\blacktriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}\to-)\cong\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\blacktriangleleft u\to-)
.

Proof.

Since
{\mathcal{C}}
is tensored and the representable functors
{\mathcal{C}}(a\to-)
and
{\mathcal{C}}(a\vartriangleleft u\to-)
are both tensored, we get the following natural isomorphism of representable functors:

(5.6)

\begin{split}\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\vartriangleleft u\blacktriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}\to b\blacktriangleleft v)&={\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}(a\vartriangleleft u\blacktriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}\to b\blacktriangleleft v))\\
&={\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\vartriangleleft u\to b)v))\\
&={\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\vartriangleleft u\to b)v)\\
&\cong{\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\vartriangleleft u\to b\vartriangleleft v))\\
&={\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\vartriangleleft u\to b\vartriangleleft v)\\
&\cong{\mathcal{V}}(u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b\vartriangleleft v))\\
&\cong{\mathcal{V}}(u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)v)\\
&\cong{\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)v))\\
&={\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}(a\blacktriangleleft u\to b\blacktriangleleft v))\\
&=\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\blacktriangleleft u\to b\blacktriangleleft v).\end{split}

To show the above isomorphism is natural in
b\blacktriangleleft v
, one uses the naturality condition (
5.5
) twice.
We use (
5.5
) the first time for
\mu^{{\mathcal{C}}(a\vartriangleleft u\to-)}_{b,v}
in the first isomophism in (
5.6
).
For the second use, notice that when
{\mathcal{C}}(a\to-)
tensored, (
5.5
) is equivalent to

[\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b\vartriangleleft v)}(\mathbf{1}_{b}\vartriangleleft g)\alpha^{\mathcal{C}}_{b,{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c),w}(\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{b\to c}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{w})]\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)\circ\mu_{c,w}^{-1}=\mu_{b,v}^{-1}\circ(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}g)\circ((-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)\mathbf{1}_{w}).

We use this equivalent version of (
5.5
) in the third isomorphism in (
5.6
).
We leave the rest of the details to the reader.
∎

Theorem 5.12
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{C}}
is a tensored
{\mathcal{V}}
-category.
The following are equivalent:

(1)

Every
{\mathcal{V}}
-representable functor
{\mathcal{R}}^{a}={\mathcal{C}}(a\to-):{\mathcal{C}}\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}
is tensored.

(2)

The
{\mathcal{V}}
-functor
{\mathcal{I}}:{\mathcal{C}}\to\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}
given by
a\mapsto a\blacktriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}
from Definition
5.5
is tensored.

(3)

The
1_{\mathcal{V}}
-graded natural transformation
\tau:\mathbf{1}^{\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}}\Rightarrow\overline{\mathbf{1}^{\mathcal{C}}}\circ{\mathcal{I}}
defined by

\tau_{a\blacktriangleleft u}:=\mu^{\mathcal{I}}_{a,u}
from Lemma
5.9
is a natural isomorphism.

(4)

The
{\mathcal{V}}
-functors
{\mathcal{I}}:{\mathcal{C}}\to\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}
given by
a\mapsto a\blacktriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}
from Definition
5.5
and

\overline{\mathbf{1}^{\mathcal{C}}}:\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
given by
a\blacktriangleleft u\mapsto a\vartriangleleft u

from Proposition
5.7
witness a
{\mathcal{V}}
-equivalence.

Proof.

First suppose (1) holds.
Notice that
{\mathcal{I}}(a)\vartriangleleft u=a\blacktriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}\vartriangleleft u=a\blacktriangleleft u
and

{\mathcal{I}}(a\vartriangleleft u)=a\vartriangleleft u\blacktriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}
.
Since
{\mathcal{C}}
is tensored and every
{\mathcal{V}}
-representable functor is tensored, Proposition
5.11
gives us a canonical natural isomorphism of representable functors
\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\vartriangleleft u\blacktriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}\to-)\cong\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\blacktriangleleft u\to-)
.
By the Yoneda Lemma as in (
2.6
), we get a canonical isomorphism in
\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\blacktriangleleft u\to a\vartriangleleft u\blacktriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}})
.
We claim this isomorphism is exactly
\mu^{\mathcal{I}}_{a,u}
, which is thus invertible by (
2.6
).
Indeed, setting
b=a\vartriangleleft u
and
v=1_{\mathcal{V}}
,
Adjunction (
5.6
) becomes the following isomorphism:

\displaystyle\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\vartriangleleft u\blacktriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}\to a\vartriangleleft u\blacktriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}})

\displaystyle={\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\vartriangleleft u\to a\vartriangleleft u))

\displaystyle={\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\vartriangleleft u\to a\vartriangleleft u)

\displaystyle\cong{\mathcal{V}}(u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to a\vartriangleleft u))

\displaystyle={\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to a\vartriangleleft u)))

\displaystyle=\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\blacktriangleleft u\to a\vartriangleleft u\blacktriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}).

Moreover,
\mathbf{1}_{a\vartriangleleft u\blacktriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}}
transforms as follows under the above isomorphism:

\mathbf{1}_{a\vartriangleleft u\blacktriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}}=j_{a\vartriangleleft u}\leftrightarrow\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{a,u}\underset{\text{\eqref{eq:Mu=Eta}}}{=}\mu^{\mathcal{I}}_{a,u}.

The condition that
{\mathcal{I}}
is tensored is exactly the condition that
\mu^{\mathcal{I}}_{a,u}
is invertible for all
a\in{\mathcal{C}}
and
u\in{\mathcal{V}}
, which is equivalent to
\tau_{a\blacktriangleleft u}
being invertible for all
a\blacktriangleleft u\in\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}
.
Hence
{\mathcal{I}}
is tensored if and only if
\tau:\mathbf{1}^{\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}}\Rightarrow\overline{\mathbf{1}^{\mathcal{C}}}\circ{\mathcal{I}}
is an isomorphism.

Suppose
{\mathcal{I}}
is tensored, so
\tau:\mathbf{1}^{\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}}\Rightarrow\overline{\mathbf{1}^{\mathcal{C}}}\circ{\mathcal{I}}
is an isomorphism.
Since we always have an equivalence of
{\mathcal{V}}
-functors
{\mathcal{I}}\circ\overline{\mathbf{1}^{\mathcal{C}}}\cong\mathbf{1}^{\mathcal{C}}
by Proposition
5.7
, we have a
{\mathcal{V}}
-equivalence
{\mathcal{C}}\cong\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}
.

Assume (4) holds.
Then for all
v\in{\mathcal{V}}
and
a,b\in{\mathcal{C}}
, the following chain of isomorphisms is natural in
u\in{\mathcal{V}}
:

\displaystyle{\mathcal{V}}(u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)v)

\displaystyle\cong{\mathcal{V}}(u\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)v))

\displaystyle={\mathcal{V}}(u\to\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}(a\blacktriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}\to b\blacktriangleleft v)

\displaystyle\cong{\mathcal{V}}(u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}\to b\vartriangleleft v))

\displaystyle\cong{\mathcal{V}}(u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b\vartriangleleft v))

Hence we have an isomorphism of representable functors
{\mathcal{V}}(-\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)v)\cong{\mathcal{V}}(-\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b\vartriangleleft v))
, which gives us an isomorphism
{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)v\cong{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b\vartriangleleft v)
.
We claim this isomorphism is exactly
\mu^{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to-)}_{b,v}
.
Indeed, setting
u={\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)v
and passing through the above chain of isomorphisms,
\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)v}
transforms as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)v}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\eta^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{1_{\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)v}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\eta^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{1_{\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)v}\circ\overline{\mathbf{1}^{\mathcal{C}}}_{a\blacktriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}\to b\blacktriangleleft v}

\displaystyle\leftrightarrow\eta^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{1_{\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)v}\circ\overline{\mathbf{1}^{\mathcal{C}}}_{a\blacktriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}\to b\blacktriangleleft v}\circ(\rho_{a}^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}\to b\vartriangleleft v)})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)

\displaystyle\underset{\text{Lem.~\ref{lem:StrictlyUnital}}}{=}\eta^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{1_{\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)v}\circ\overline{\mathbf{1}^{\mathcal{C}}}_{a\blacktriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}\to b\blacktriangleleft v}\circ(\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{a,1}\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}}\to b\vartriangleleft v)})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)

\displaystyle=\eta^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{1_{\mathcal{V}},{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)v}\circ\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)v}\circ(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{b,v})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{b,v})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)

\displaystyle\underset{\text{Lem.~\ref{lem:NaturalityForRepresentableFunctor}}}{=}\mu^{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to-)}_{b,v}.

Hence
\mu^{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to-)}_{b,v}
is an isomorphism by the Yoneda Lemma as in (
2.6
).
∎

5.4.
Completion and rigidity of
{\mathcal{V}}

We now describe the connection between tensored
{\mathcal{V}}
-functors and the notion of rigidity.
As before, we assume
{\mathcal{V}}
is closed.

Example 5.13
.

When
{\mathcal{V}}
is rigid,
our convention for duals is given as in
[
MP17
, §2.7]
by
\ev_{v}\in{\mathcal{V}}(vv^{*}\to 1_{\mathcal{V}})
and
\coev_{v}\in{\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to v^{*}v)
.
Hence
{\mathcal{V}}
is closed with
\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to v)=u^{*}v
.
Notice that
\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to v)w=u^{*}vw=\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to vw)
for all
u,v,w\in{\mathcal{V}}
, so every
{\mathcal{V}}
-representable functor
\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to-)
is clearly tensored.
In fact, we show this property
characterizes
rigidity in Lemma
5.14
below.

We can now describe
\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}
in more detail without taking any mates.
The identity element
j_{v}\in{\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to[v,v])={\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to v^{*}v)
for
v\in\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}
is equal to
\coev_{v}
.
The composition morphism
-\circ_{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}-\in{\mathcal{V}}(\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to v)\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(v\to w)\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to w))={\mathcal{V}}(u^{*}vv^{*}w\to u^{*}w)
is equal to
\mathbf{1}_{u^{*}}\ev_{v}\mathbf{1}_{w}
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{C}}
is a
{\mathcal{V}}
-category with
{\mathcal{V}}
rigid.
The
{\mathcal{V}}
-representable functor
{\mathcal{C}}(a\to-):{\mathcal{C}}\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}
is given by

{\mathcal{C}}(a\to-)_{b\to c}=(\coev_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c)})\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)\in{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{C}}(b\to c)\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)^{*}{\mathcal{C}}(a\to c)).

We now describe the tensored
{\mathcal{V}}
-category
\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}
in greater detail.
We now have
\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}(a\blacktriangleleft u\to b\blacktriangleleft v)=\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)v)=u^{*}{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)v
, and the identity elements and composition are given by
j_{a\blacktriangleleft u}=\coev_{u}\circ(\mathbf{1}_{u^{*}}j_{a}\mathbf{1}_{u})\in{\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to u^{*}{\mathcal{C}}(a\to a)u)
and

-\circ_{\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}}-=(\mathbf{1}_{u^{*}}\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}\ev_{v}\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c)}\mathbf{1}_{w})\circ(\mathbf{1}_{u^{*}}(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)\mathbf{1}_{w})\in{\mathcal{V}}(u^{*}{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)vv^{*}{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c)w\to u^{*}{\mathcal{C}}(a\to c)w).

Moreover, the left
{\mathcal{V}}
-adjoint
{\mathcal{L}}^{a\blacktriangleleft u}
of
{\mathcal{R}}^{a\blacktriangleleft u}
is given by

{\mathcal{L}}_{v\to w}^{a\blacktriangleleft u}=(\mathbf{1}_{v^{*}}\coev_{u}\mathbf{1}_{w})\circ(\mathbf{1}_{v^{*}}\mathbf{1}_{u^{*}}j_{a}\mathbf{1}_{u}\mathbf{1}_{w})\in{\mathcal{V}}(v^{*}w\to v^{*}u^{*}{\mathcal{C}}(a\to a)uw^{*})

Finally, the
{\mathcal{V}}
-functor

{\mathcal{I}}:{\mathcal{C}}\to\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is given by
{\mathcal{I}}_{a\to b}=\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}\in{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)\to 1_{\mathcal{V}}^{*}{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)1_{\mathcal{V}})
.

Lemma 5.14
.

Consider the self-enriched
{\mathcal{V}}
-category
\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}
.
Every
{\mathcal{V}}
-representable functor
\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(v\to-)
is tensored if and only if every object of
{\mathcal{V}}
has a right dual.

Remark 5.15
.

This result follows from
[
GP18
, Proposition 6.2]
, going back to
[
Str83
]
. We give an explicit proof here for the reader’s convenience.

Proof.

If
{\mathcal{V}}
is rigid, then
\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to v):=u^{*}v
, so
\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to-)=u^{*}\otimes-
which is obviously tensored.

Conversely, suppose that for
v\in{\mathcal{V}}
,
\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(v\to-)
is tensored.
We define
v^{*}:=\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(v\to 1_{\mathcal{V}})
and

\displaystyle\ev_{v}

\displaystyle:=\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{v\to 1_{\mathcal{V}}}\in{\mathcal{V}}(v\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(v\to 1_{\mathcal{V}})\to 1_{\mathcal{V}})

\displaystyle\coev_{v}

\displaystyle:=j_{v}\circ(\mu^{{\mathcal{V}}(v\to-)}_{1_{\mathcal{V}},v})^{-1}\in{\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(v\to 1_{\mathcal{V}})v)

One zig-zag relation is readily verified using standard mate calculations:

(\mathbf{1}_{v}\coev_{v})\circ(\ev_{v}\mathbf{1}_{v})=(\mathbf{1}_{v}j_{v})\circ(\mathbf{1}_{v}(\mu^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(v\to-)}_{v,1_{\mathcal{V}}})^{-1})\circ(\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{v\to 1_{\mathcal{V}}}\mathbf{1}_{v})=(\mathbf{1}_{v}j_{v})\circ\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{v\to v}=\mathbf{1}_{v}

The second is a bit more difficult.
First, one shows that
(\eta^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{v,1_{\mathcal{V}}}\mathbf{1}_{v^{*}})\circ(-\circ_{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}-)=\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{v\to 1_{\mathcal{V}}}\circ j_{1_{\mathcal{V}}}
by taking mates under the adjunction

{\mathcal{V}}(v\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(v\to 1_{\mathcal{V}})\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to 1_{\mathcal{V}}))\cong{\mathcal{V}}(c\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(v\to 1_{\mathcal{V}})\to 1_{\mathcal{V}}),

It follows that
((\mu^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(v\to-)}_{v,1_{\mathcal{V}}})^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{v^{*}})\circ(\mathbf{1}_{v^{*}}\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{v\to 1})=-\circ_{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}-\in{\mathcal{V}}(\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(v\to v)\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(v\to 1_{\mathcal{V}})\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(v\to 1_{\mathcal{V}}))
.
Hence

(\coev_{v}\mathbf{1}_{v^{*}})\circ(\mathbf{1}_{v^{*}}\ev_{v})=(j_{v}\mathbf{1}_{v^{*}})\circ((\mu^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(v\to-)}_{v,1_{\mathcal{V}}})^{-1}\mathbf{1}_{v^{*}})\circ(\mathbf{1}_{v^{*}}\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{v\to 1_{\mathcal{V}}})=(j_{v}\mathbf{1}_{v^{*}})\circ(-\circ_{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}-)=\mathbf{1}_{v^{*}}.

This completes the proof.
∎

Corollary 5.16
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{V}}
is rigid.
Then every
{\mathcal{V}}
-representable functor
{\mathcal{R}}^{a\blacktriangleleft u}:\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}
is tensored, and
\overline{\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}}
is
{\mathcal{V}}
-equivalent to
\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}
by Theorem
5.12
.

Proof.

When
{\mathcal{V}}
is rigid,

\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}(a\blacktriangleleft u\to b\blacktriangleleft v)=\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)v)=u^{*}{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)v=\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}(a\blacktriangleleft u\to b\blacktriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}})v.

We leave the rest of the details to the reader.
∎

Corollary 5.17
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{V}}
is rigid and
{\mathcal{C}}
is tensored.
Then
{\mathcal{I}}:{\mathcal{C}}\to\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}
is tensored,
and
{\mathcal{C}}
is
{\mathcal{V}}
-equivalent to
\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}
by Theorem
5.12
.

Proof.

Notice that when
{\mathcal{C}}
is tensored and
{\mathcal{V}}
is rigid, we have an adjunction

(5.7)

\begin{split}\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\blacktriangleleft u\to b\blacktriangleleft v)&={\mathcal{V}}(u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)v)\\
&\cong{\mathcal{V}}(uv^{*}\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b))\\
&\cong{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\vartriangleleft uv^{*}\to b)\\
&\cong{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\vartriangleleft u\to b\vartriangleleft v).\end{split}

Define
\Phi:\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}^{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}
by
\Phi(a\blacktriangleleft u)=a\vartriangleleft u
, and on morphisms,
\Phi
is defined by the adjunction (
5.7
).

We claim that
\Phi
is an equivalence of categories.
It is clear that if
\Phi
is a functor, then
\Phi
is an equivalence of categories, since it is automatically essentially surjective on objects by definition and fully faithful by (
5.7
).
Setting
b=a
and
v=u
, it is readily checked that
\Phi(\mathbf{1}_{a\blacktriangleleft u})=\mathbf{1}_{a\vartriangleleft u}
.
Hence for an arbitrary
f\in\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a\blacktriangleleft u\to b\blacktriangleleft v)={\mathcal{V}}(u\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)v)
,
we have
\Phi(f)=(\mathbf{1}_{a}\vartriangleleft f)\circ\alpha_{a,{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b),v}\circ(\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{a\to b}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{v})
.
Now if

g\in\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}^{\mathcal{V}}(b\blacktriangleleft v\to c\blacktriangleleft w)={\mathcal{V}}(v\to{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c)w)
,
we see that

\displaystyle\Phi

\displaystyle(f\circ g)

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{1}_{a}\vartriangleleft[f\circ(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}g)\circ((-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)\mathbf{1}_{w})])\circ\alpha_{a,{\mathcal{C}}(a\to c),w}\circ(\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{a\to c}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{w})

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{1}_{a}\vartriangleleft[f\circ(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}g)])\circ\alpha_{a,{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b){\mathcal{C}}(b\to c),w}\circ(\alpha_{a,{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b),{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c)}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{w})\circ(\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{a\to b}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c)}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{w})\circ(\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{b\to c}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{w})

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{1}_{a}\vartriangleleft[f\circ(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}g)])\circ\alpha_{a,{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b),{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c)w}\circ\alpha_{a\vartriangleleft{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b),{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c),w}\circ(\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{a\to b}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c)}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{w})\circ(\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{b\to c}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{w})

\displaystyle=(\mathbf{1}_{a}\vartriangleleft f)\circ\alpha_{a,{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b),v}\circ(\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{a\to b}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{v})\circ(\mathbf{1}_{b}\vartriangleleft g)\circ\alpha_{b,{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c),w}\circ(\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{b\to c}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{w})

\displaystyle=\Phi(f)\circ\Phi(g).

Thus
\Phi
is a functor, and it gives an equivalence of categories
\overline{{\mathcal{C}}}^{\mathcal{V}}\cong{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}
.

We now claim that setting
b=a\vartriangleleft u
and
v=1_{\mathcal{V}}
,
\Phi(\mu^{\mathcal{I}}_{a,u})=\alpha_{a,u,1_{\mathcal{V}}}
.
Indeed,
using the identity
\mu^{\mathcal{I}}_{a,u}=\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{a,u}
from (
5.4
), we have

\Phi(\mu^{\mathcal{I}}_{a,u})=(\mathbf{1}_{a}\vartriangleleft\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{a,u})\circ\alpha_{a,{\mathcal{C}}(a\to a\vartriangleleft u),1_{\mathcal{V}}}\circ(\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{a\to a\vartriangleleft u}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{1_{\mathcal{V}}})=\alpha_{a,u,1_{\mathcal{V}}}\circ(\mathbf{1}_{a}\vartriangleleft\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{v}\vartriangleleft\mathbf{1}_{1_{\mathcal{V}}})\circ(\varepsilon^{\mathcal{C}}_{a\to a\vartriangleleft u}\vartriangleleft 1_{\mathcal{V}})=\alpha_{a,u,1_{\mathcal{V}}}.

Since
\Phi
is an equivalence of categories,
\Phi(\mu^{\mathcal{I}}_{a,u})=\alpha_{a,u,1_{\mathcal{V}}}
being invertible implies
\mu^{\mathcal{I}}_{a,u}
is invertible.
Hence
{\mathcal{I}}
is tensored, and we are finished.
∎

6.
Closed
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal categories

For the remainder of this article,
{\mathcal{V}}
will denote a braided monoidal category.

6.1.
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal categories

We now recall the basics of (strict)
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal categories from
[
MP17
]
.

Definition 6.1
.

A (strict)
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal category consists of a
{\mathcal{V}}
-category
{\mathcal{C}}
, together with the following additional data:

•

(identity object) a distinguished object
1_{\mathcal{C}}\in{\mathcal{C}}

•

(tensor product of objects) for all
a,b\in{\mathcal{C}}
, an object
ab\in{\mathcal{C}}

•

(tensor product for hom-objects) for all
a,b,c,d\in{\mathcal{C}}
, a distinguished morphism
-\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}-\in{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{C}}(a\to b){\mathcal{C}}(c\to d)\to{\mathcal{C}}(ac\to bd))
.

subject to the following axioms:

•

(strict unitality for objects) for all
a\in{\mathcal{C}}
,
1_{\mathcal{C}}a=a=a1_{\mathcal{C}}
.

•

(strict associator for objects) for all
a,b,c,\in{\mathcal{C}}
,
a(bc)=(ab)c
.

•

(unitality) for all
a,b\in{\mathcal{C}}
,
(j_{1_{\mathcal{C}}}\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)})\circ(-\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}-)=\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b}=(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)}j_{1_{\mathcal{C}}})\circ(-\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}-)
and
(j_{a}j_{b})\circ(-\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}-)=j_{ab}
,

•

(associatitivity)
as morphisms in
{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{C}}(a\to b){\mathcal{C}}(c\to d){\mathcal{C}}(e\to f)\to{\mathcal{C}}(ace\to bdf))
, we have

(\mathbf{1}(-\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}-))\circ(-\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}-)=((-\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}-)\mathbf{1})\circ(-\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}-)
,
and

•

(braided interchange) for all
a,b,c,d,e,f
, the following diagram commutes:

(6.1)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b){\mathcal{C}}(d\to e){\mathcal{C}}(b\to c){\mathcal{C}}(e\to f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}(ad\to be){\mathcal{C}}(be\to cf)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}(ad\to ef)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b){\mathcal{C}}(b\to c){\mathcal{C}}(d\to e){\mathcal{C}}(e\to f)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}(a\to c){\mathcal{C}}(d\to f)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}-)(-\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{1}\beta_{{\mathcal{C}}(d\to e),{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c)}\mathbf{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}-}

where
\beta
is the braiding in
{\mathcal{V}}
.

It is straightforward to verify that the underlying category
{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}
of a
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal category is a monoidal category.

Example 6.2
.

When
{\mathcal{V}}
is braided closed monoidal, can make
\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}
a
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal category by defining
-\otimes_{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}-
to be the mate of
(\mathbf{1}_{u}\beta_{w,[u,v]}\mathbf{1}_{x})\circ(\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{u\to v}\varepsilon^{\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}}_{w\to x})
under the adjunction

{\mathcal{V}}(\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to v)\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(w\to x)\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(uw\to vx))\cong{\mathcal{V}}(uw\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(u\to v)\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}(w\to x)\to vx).

We leave the details to the reader as a valuable exercise.

Definition 6.3

(
[
MP17
]
)
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}}
are
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal categories.
A (strong) strongly unital
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal functor
{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{D}}
consists of a pair
({\mathcal{F}},\nu)
where
{\mathcal{F}}:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{D}}
is a
{\mathcal{V}}
-functor such that
{\mathcal{F}}(1_{\mathcal{C}})=1_{\mathcal{D}}
and
{\mathcal{F}}_{1_{\mathcal{C}}\to 1_{\mathcal{C}}}=j_{1_{\mathcal{D}}}
,
and

\nu=(\nu_{a,b})_{a,b\in{\mathcal{C}}}
is family of isomorphisms
\nu_{a,b}\in{\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{F}}(ab)\to{\mathcal{F}}(a){\mathcal{F}}(b)))
which satisfy the following conditions:

•

(unitality)
For all
a\in{\mathcal{C}}
,
\nu_{a,1_{\mathcal{C}}}=\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{F}}(a)}=\nu_{1_{\mathcal{C}},a}
,
5
5

5

The axiom that
\nu_{a,1_{\mathcal{C}}}=\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{F}}(a)}=\nu_{1_{\mathcal{C}},a}
for all
a\in{\mathcal{C}}
is not stated in
[
MP17
, Def. 2.6]
; rather only the condition that
\nu_{1_{\mathcal{C}},1_{\mathcal{C}}}=j_{1_{\mathcal{D}}}
appears.
This was an error, as this axiom is used throughout, and its verification was omitted (this gap is covered here).
See also Lemma
3.3
and Remark
6.11
.

•

(associativity)
for all
a,b,c\in{\mathcal{C}}
, as composites in
{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{F}}(abc)\to{\mathcal{F}}(a){\mathcal{F}}(b){\mathcal{F}}(c))
,
\nu_{a,bc}\circ(\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{F}}(a)}\nu_{b,c})=\nu_{ab,c}\circ(\nu_{a,b}\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{F}}(c)})
,

•

(naturality)

(6.2)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}(a\to c){\mathcal{C}}(b\to d)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{C}}(ab\to cd)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{F}}(a)\to{\mathcal{F}}(c)){\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{F}}(b)\to{\mathcal{F}}(d))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{F}}(ab)\to{\mathcal{F}}(cd))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{F}}(a){\mathcal{F}}(b)\to{\mathcal{F}}(c){\mathcal{F}}(d))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{F}}(ab)\to{\mathcal{F}}(c){\mathcal{F}}(d))}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}_{a\to c}{\mathcal{F}}_{b\to d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{F}}_{ab\to cd}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\otimes_{\mathcal{D}}-}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces-\circ\nu_{c,d}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nu_{a,b}\circ-}

We leave the definition of a lax/oplax
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal functor to the reader.
Note that the underlying functor of a strong/lax/oplax
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal functor is strong/lax/oplax monoidal with the same tensorator/laxitor/oplaxitor.

Definition 6.4
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}}
are
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal categories and
({\mathcal{F}},\nu^{\mathcal{F}}),({\mathcal{G}},\nu^{\mathcal{G}}):{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{D}}
are
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal functors.
A strongly unital
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal natural transformation
\sigma:({\mathcal{F}},\nu^{\mathcal{F}})\Rightarrow({\mathcal{G}},\nu^{\mathcal{G}})
is a
1_{\mathcal{V}}
-graded natural transformation
\sigma:{\mathcal{F}}\Rightarrow{\mathcal{G}}
satisfying the naturality condition (
2.2
) and the additional axioms

•

(unitality)
\sigma_{1_{\mathcal{C}}}\in{\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{D}}({\mathcal{F}}(1_{\mathcal{C}})\to{\mathcal{G}}(1_{\mathcal{C}})))={\mathcal{V}}(1_{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{D}}(1_{\mathcal{D}}\to 1_{\mathcal{D}}))
is equal to
j_{1_{\mathcal{D}}}
,

•

(monoidality)
for all
a,b\in{\mathcal{C}}
, as composites in
{\mathcal{D}}^{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{F}}(ab)\to{\mathcal{G}}(a){\mathcal{G}}(b))
,

\sigma_{ab}\circ\nu^{\mathcal{G}}_{a,b}=\nu^{\mathcal{F}}_{a,b}\circ(\sigma_{a}\sigma_{b})
.

As before, we denote by
{\mathcal{R}}_{a}:{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{V}}
the representable functor
b\mapsto{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b)
from Example
2.13
.
When
{\mathcal{V}}
is closed, we denote by
{\mathcal{R}}^{a}:{\mathcal{C}}\to\widehat{{\mathcal{V}}}
the
{\mathcal{V}}
-representable functor from Example
2.8
.

6.2.
Closed
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal categories

Example 6.5
.

Suppose
{\mathcal{C}}
is
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal and
a\in{\mathcal{C}}
.
We can define a
{\mathcal{V}}
-functor
a\otimes-:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
on objects by
a\otimes b=ab
and
(a\otimes-)_{b\to c}\in{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{C}}(b\to c)\to{\mathcal{C}}(ab\to ac))
is
(j_{a}\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(b\to c)})\circ(-\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}-)
.
It is straightforward to verify
a\otimes-
is a
{\mathcal{V}}
-functor using the braided interchange relation.

Notice that the underlying functor is given by
a\otimes-:{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}
, where
a\otimes b=ab
and for
f\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(b\to c)
,
a\otimes f=\mathbf{1}_{a}\otimes f\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(ab\to ac)
.

Definition 6.6
.

We call a
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal category
{\mathcal{C}}

closed
if every
{\mathcal{V}}
-functor
a\otimes-
has a right
{\mathcal{V}}
-adjoint, denoted
[a,-]:{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
.

Example 6.7
.

Recall that a
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal category is called
rigid
if the underlying monoidal category
{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}
is rigid.
As in
[
MP17
]
, to ease the notation, we assume
(ba)^{*}=a^{*}b^{*}
for all
a,b\in{\mathcal{C}}
.

Notice that a rigid
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal category is closed with
[a,-]=a^{*}\otimes-
, where the
{\mathcal{V}}
-adjunction is witnessed via the Frobenius reciprocity isomorphisms

\theta_{b,c}=[(\coev_{a}j_{b})\circ(-\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}-)][(j_{a^{*}}\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{C}}(ab\to c)})\circ(-\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}-)]\circ(-\circ_{\mathcal{C}}-)\in{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{C}}(ab\to c)\to{\mathcal{C}}(b\to a^{*}c)).

(It is an important exercise using the braided interchange relation to verify the above morphism is a natural isomorphism with the obvious inverse.)

Lemma 6.8
.

If
{\mathcal{C}}
is closed, then
{\mathcal{C}}
is oplax tensored if and only if
R_{1_{\mathcal{C}}}
admits a left adjoint.

Proof.

If
{\mathcal{R}}_{1_{\mathcal{C}}}:{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{V}}
admits a left adjoint
{\mathcal{F}}:{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}
, we get a left adjoint
{\mathcal{L}}_{a}:{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}
by
v\mapsto a{\mathcal{F}}(v)
:

(6.3)

\begin{split}{\mathcal{V}}(v\to{\mathcal{R}}_{a}(b))&={\mathcal{V}}(v\to{\mathcal{C}}(a\to b))\cong{\mathcal{V}}(v\to{\mathcal{C}}(1_{\mathcal{C}}\to[a,b]))\\
&\cong{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{F}}(v)\to[a,b])\cong{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}(a{\mathcal{F}}(v)\to b)=:{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{L}}_{a}(v)\to b).\end{split}

The other direction is trivial.
∎

6.3.
Classification of rigid
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal categories

In
[
MP17
]
, we proved a classification theorem for rigid
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal categories, which is an analog of Theorem
1.4
.

Theorem 6.9

(
[
MP17
, Thm. 1.1]
)
.

Let
{\mathcal{V}}
be a braided monoidal category.
There is a bijective correspondence

\left\{\,\parbox{199.16928pt}{\rm Rigid ${\mathcal{V}}$-monoidal categories ${\mathcal{C}}$ such that ${\mathcal{R}}_{1_{\mathcal{C}}}:{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{V}}$ admits a left adjoint}\,\right\}\,\,\cong\,\,\left\{\,\parbox{227.62204pt}{\rm Pairs $({\mathcal{T}},{\mathcal{F}}^{\scriptscriptstyle Z})$ with ${\mathcal{T}}$ a rigid monoidal category and ${\mathcal{F}}^{\scriptscriptstyle Z}:{\mathcal{V}}\to Z({\mathcal{T}})$ braided oplax monoidal, such that ${\mathcal{F}}:={\mathcal{F}}^{\scriptscriptstyle Z}\circ R$ admits a right adjoint}\,\right\}.

Here,
R:Z({\mathcal{T}})\to{\mathcal{T}}
denotes the forgetful functor.

Remark 6.10
.

As in
[
MP17
]
, we abuse nomenclature by assuming our oplax monoidal functors
({\mathcal{F}},\nu):{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{T}}
are strongly unital; that is

{\mathcal{F}}(1_{\mathcal{V}})=1_{\mathcal{T}}
and
\nu_{v,1_{\mathcal{V}}}=\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{F}}(v)}=\nu_{1_{\mathcal{V}},v}
for all
v\in{\mathcal{V}}
.
See also Remark
6.11
.

6.4.
Classification of closed
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal categories

It is straightforward to generalize Theorem
6.9
, relaxing rigidity on both sides to closed, obtaining a bijective correspondence between closed
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal categories and pairs
({\mathcal{T}},{\mathcal{F}}^{\scriptscriptstyle Z})
with
{\mathcal{T}}
closed.
This is a better analog of Theorem
1.4
than Theorem
6.9
.

Theorem

(Thm.
1.1
)
.

Let
{\mathcal{V}}
be a braided monoidal category.
There is a bijective correspondence

\left\{\,\parbox{199.16928pt}{\rm Closed ${\mathcal{V}}$-monoidal categories ${\mathcal{C}}$ such that ${\mathcal{R}}_{1_{\mathcal{C}}}$ admits a left adjoint}\,\right\}\,\,\cong\,\,\left\{\,\parbox{236.15796pt}{\rm Pairs $({\mathcal{T}},{\mathcal{F}}^{\scriptscriptstyle Z})$ with ${\mathcal{T}}$ a closed monoidal category and ${\mathcal{F}}^{\scriptscriptstyle Z}:{\mathcal{V}}\to Z({\mathcal{T}})$ braided oplax monoidal, such that ${\mathcal{F}}:={\mathcal{F}}^{\scriptscriptstyle Z}\circ R$ admits a right adjoint}\,\right\}.

We now give a brief description of both directions, together with a proof of the essential lemmas needed when
{\mathcal{C}}
(respectively
{\mathcal{T}}
) is closed rather than rigid.

Starting with a
{\mathcal{V}}
-monoidal
{\mathcal{C}}
such that
{\mathcal{R}}_{1_{\mathcal{V}}}:{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{V}}
admits a left adjoint
{\mathcal{F}}:{\mathcal{V}}\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}
, we let
\eta
be the unit of the adjunction.
We see that
{\mathcal{F}}
can be endowed with the structure of a strongly unital oplax monoidal functor by defining
\nu_{u,v}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{F}}(uv)\to{\mathcal{F}}(u){\mathcal{F}}(v))
to be the mate of

(\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{1_{\mathcal{C}},u}\eta^{\mathcal{C}}_{1_{\mathcal{C}},v})\circ(-\otimes_{\mathcal{C}}-)
under the adjunction

{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}({\mathcal{F}}(uv)\to{\mathcal{F}}(u){\mathcal{F}}(v))\cong{\mathcal{V}}(uv\to{\mathcal{C}}(1_{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{F}}(u){\mathcal{F}}(v))).

Remark 6.11
.

In
[
MP17
, Lem. 4.4]
, it was shown that
{\mathcal{F}}(1_{\mathcal{V}})
is canonically isomorphic to
1_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}}
via the Yoneda Lemma, and thus we may identify
{\mathcal{F}}(1_{\mathcal{V}})=1_{{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathcal{V}}}
.
That paper (in error!) omitted to show the easily verified that under this identification,
\nu_{1_{\mathcal{V}},v}=\mathbf{1}_{{\mathcal{F}}(v)}=\nu_{v,1_{\mathcal{V}}}
for all
v\in{\mathcal{V}}
.
Indeed, the verification is the same as the proof of Lemma
3.3
.

Since
